<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:criteria}
Three accuracy criteria appear below: \emph{(i) expected residual/distance}, $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-T\hat x}\le\varepsilon$ or $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon$; \emph{(ii) expected squared distance}, $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-x^*}^2\le C\varepsilon^2$; and \emph{(iii) high probability}, $\mathbb{P}(\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon)\ge1-\vartheta$. By Jensen, (ii) implies (i) up to a factor $\sqrt C$, and iterating any (iii)-guarantee $O(\log(1/\vartheta'))$ times at target $\varepsilon/2$ and taking a median yields (i) with a constant-factor increase in cost, provided the error is bounded on the failure event --- as holds throughout this paper, where every iterate satisfies $\norm{x_n-x^*}\le D+\norm{x_n-\hat x_n}$ with the deviation controlled by Lemma~\ref{lem:master} and $\norm{\hat x_n-x^*}\le D$ on the exact path. All complexity statements below are explicit about which criterion is used, and the conversions are used only where stated.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
$D=\norm{x_0-x^*}$; $\sigma^2$ the per-evaluation variance bound; $\varepsilon$ the target; $\tilde O$ hides factors logarithmic in $1/\varepsilon$, $1/\delta$, $D$, and $1/(1-\gamma)$. All Hilbert spaces are real; inner products are denoted $\langle \cdot,\cdot \rangle$. Terminology for nonexpansive and averaged operators follows \cite{BauschkeCombettes2017}.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:oracle}
Each evaluation of $\widehat T$ at $x$ returns $T(x)+\xi$ where $\mathbb{E}[\xi]=0$ and $\mathbb{E}\norm{\xi}^2\le\sigma^2$. Evaluations are independent across calls. A \emph{batch} of $m$ evaluations at $x$ returns the average $\widehat T_m(x)$ with $\mathbb{E}[\widehat T_m(x)]=T(x)$ and $\mathbb{E}\norm{\widehat T_m(x)-T(x)}^2\le\sigma^2/m$. For the lower bounds of Section~\ref{sec:contraction} the hard instances use Gaussian noise $\xi\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2)$ (or $\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2 I)$, understood coordinate-wise), which the oracle model permits.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:filtration}
$\mathcal F_0=\sigma(x_0)$ and $\mathcal F_n$ is the $\sigma$-algebra generated by $x_0$ and all oracle responses up to and including step $n$. The iterate $x_n$ of \eqref{eq:anchored} is $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable; the batch noise $\bar\xi_n:=\widehat T_{m_n}(x_n)-T(x_n)$ is $\mathcal F_n$-measurable with $\mathbb{E}[\bar\xi_n\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]=0$ and $\mathbb{E}[\norm{\bar\xi_n}^2\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]\le\sigma^2/m_n$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:discipline}
Throughout, stochastic recursions are kept in \emph{squared norms}, expanded before any triangle inequality, with coefficients that are deterministic (or copied from a deterministic comparison path) and bounded \emph{pointwise} by deterministic envelopes. Two consequences are used constantly: (i) cross terms $\langle u_{n-1},\bar\xi_n \rangle$ with $u_{n-1}$ $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable vanish in expectation; (ii) no independence between parameters and noise is ever required. Any proof step violating (i)--(ii) is flagged where it occurs.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:small-c}
Let $T$ be a $\gamma$-contraction with fixed point $x^*$, $D=\norm{x_0-x^*}\ge 4\varepsilon$, and let $(x_n)$ follow \eqref{eq:anchored} with schedule \eqref{eq:ck-schedule} and
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cstar}
c \;=\; \min\Bigl\{1,\ \frac{\varepsilon}{96D}\Bigr\}, \qquad N \;=\; \Bigl\lceil \tfrac{4\,\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))}{\ln(1/\gamma)} \Bigr\rceil .
\end{equation}
Then $\norm{x_N - x^*}\le 3\varepsilon/4$. The schedule contains no $\gamma$, and $N=O\bigl(\ln(2D/\varepsilon)/(1-\gamma)\bigr)$ for every $\gamma\in(0,1)$.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Write $e_n=\norm{x_n-x^*}$, $\delta=1-\gamma$, $L=\ln(2D/\varepsilon)$. One step of \eqref{eq:anchored} gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:onestep}
e_{n+1} \;\le\; \theta_n D + (1-\theta_n)\gamma e_n ,
\end{equation}
and unfolding \eqref{eq:onestep} exactly (with the kernel $\prod_{j=k+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)$ kept, not dropped),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unfold}
e_N \;\le\; \gamma^N r_N D \;+\; D\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}\,\gamma^{N-1-k},\qquad a_k^{(N)}:=\theta_k\!\!\prod_{j=k+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j),\quad r_N:=\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j).
\end{equation}
The definition of $N$ gives $\gamma^N D\le\bigl(\tfrac{\varepsilon\gamma}{2D}\bigr)^{4}D\le\varepsilon/2$: indeed $\gamma^N\le(\varepsilon\gamma/(2D))^4$ since $N\ln(1/\gamma)\ge4\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))$, and $(\varepsilon\gamma/(2D))^4D=\varepsilon^4\gamma^4/(16D^3)\le\varepsilon/2$ because $\gamma\le1$ and $\varepsilon\le D/4$ give $\varepsilon^4/(16D^3)=\varepsilon\cdot(\varepsilon/D)^3/16\le\varepsilon/(16\cdot64)<\varepsilon/2$. The ceiling adds at most one extra factor of $\gamma$. We first record
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ndelta}
N\delta\;\ge\;4,
\end{equation}
because $N\ge 4\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))/\ln(1/\gamma)$ and: for $\gamma\le\tfrac12$,
$(1-\gamma)\ln\tfrac{2D}{\varepsilon\gamma}=(1-\gamma)\bigl(L+\ln\tfrac1\gamma\bigr)=L+\ln\tfrac1\gamma-\gamma L-\gamma\ln\tfrac1\gamma\ge\ln\tfrac1\gamma$,
using $\gamma\ln(1/\gamma)\le1/e$ and $L(1-\gamma)\ge\tfrac12\ln 8>1>1/e$ (as $L=\ln(2D/\varepsilon)\ge\ln 8$ since $\varepsilon\le D/4$); for $\gamma\ge\tfrac12$, $\ln(1/\gamma)\le\delta/(1-\delta)\le2\delta$, so $N\delta\ge2\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))\ge2L\ge4.16$.

For the anchor sum we use the kernel upper bound, valid for all $1\le k\le N-1$ with an absolute constant: since $1-x\le e^{-x}$ and, by the standard two-sided estimate for harmonic numbers ($\ln m+\gamma_E\le H_m\le\ln m+\gamma_E+(1-\gamma_E)$ for all $m\ge1$),
$$H_{N+1}-H_{k+2}\;\ge\;\ln\tfrac{N+1}{k+2}-(1-\gamma_E),$$
hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kernel-ub}
\begin{split}
a_k^{(N)}&=\frac{c}{k+2}\prod_{j=k+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)\;\le\;\frac{c}{k+2}\,e^{-c(H_{N+1}-H_{k+2})}\\
&\le\;e^{c(1-\gamma_E)}\,\frac{c}{k+2}\Bigl(\frac{k+2}{N+1}\Bigr)^{c}\;\le\;2\,\frac{c}{k+1}\Bigl(\frac{k+1}{N+1}\Bigr)^{c}\;\le\;2\,\frac{c}{k}\Bigl(\frac{k}{N}\Bigr)^{c},
\end{split}
\end{equation}
using $e^{c(1-\gamma_E)}\le e^{1-\gamma_E}<2$ and $\frac1{k+2}\bigl(\frac{k+2}{N+1}\bigr)^c\le\frac1{k+1}\bigl(\frac{k+1}{N+1}\bigr)^c\le\frac1k\bigl(\frac{k}{N}\bigr)^c$ for $k\ge1$, $c\le1$ (both factors $(\frac{N}{N+1})^c\le1$ and $(\frac{k+2}{k+1})^{c-1}\le1$). For $k=0$: $a_0^{(N)}=\theta_0\prod_{j=1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)\le\theta_0=c/2$. For $1\le k\le N-1$ we bound \eqref{eq:kernel-ub} by a single split at $k=N/2$, applied for all $\gamma\in(0,1)$ at once. The near part $N/2\le k\le N-1$ contributes, since $k^{c-1}\le(N/2)^{c-1}=2^{1-c}N^{c-1}\le2N^{c-1}$ there,
$$2cN^{-c}\!\!\sum_{N/2\le k\le N-1}\!\!k^{c-1}\gamma^{N-1-k}\;\le\;2cN^{-c}\cdot 2N^{c-1}\sum_{j\ge0}\gamma^{j}\;=\;\frac{4c}{N\delta}\;\le\;c$$
by \eqref{eq:Ndelta}. The far part $1\le k<N/2$ contributes, since $\gamma^{N-1-k}\le\gamma^{N/2}$ there and $\sum_{k<N/2}k^{c-1}\le 1+\int_1^{N/2}x^{c-1}dx\le (N/2)^c/c$ (as $c\le1$),
$$2cN^{-c}\!\!\sum_{1\le k<N/2}\!\!k^{c-1}\gamma^{N-1-k}\;\le\;2cN^{-c}\,\gamma^{N/2}\,\frac{(N/2)^c}{c}\;=\;2^{1-c}\gamma^{N/2}\;\le\;2\gamma^{N/2}.$$
The choice of $N$ gives, for \emph{every} $\gamma\in(0,1)$ (no lower bound on $\gamma$ is required),
$$\gamma^{N/2}\;=\;e^{-\frac N2\ln(1/\gamma)}\;\le\;e^{-2\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))}\;=\;\Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon\gamma}{2D}\Bigr)^{2}\;\le\;\Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon}{2D}\Bigr)^{2},$$
hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anchor-ub}
\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}\gamma^{N-1-k}\;\le\;\frac{c}{2}+c+2\gamma^{N/2}\;\le\;\frac{3c}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2D^{2}}.
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:unfold} and \eqref{eq:anchor-ub} with $c=\varepsilon/(96D)$ and $\varepsilon\le D/4$ (so $\varepsilon^{2}/(2D)\le\varepsilon/8$),
$$e_N\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+D\Bigl(\frac{3c}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2D^{2}}\Bigr)\;=\;\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{64}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{2D}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{\varepsilon}{64}+\frac{\varepsilon}{8}\;=\;\frac{41\varepsilon}{64}\;<\;\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}.$$
Finally $N\le 4\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))/\ln(1/\gamma)+1$: for $\gamma\ge\tfrac12$, $\ln(1/\gamma)\ge\delta(1-\delta)\ge\delta/2$, so $N\le 8(L+\delta)/\delta+1$; for $\gamma\le\tfrac12$, $N\le 4(L+\ln2)/\ln2+1$ and $\delta\ge\tfrac12$; in both cases $N=O(L/\delta)$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:constants}
The constants $96$ and $4$ in \eqref{eq:cstar} are not optimized; the kernel asymptotics of Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel} show the anchor mass is $O(c\,k^{c-1}N^{-c})$ and the argument only needs $Dc=O(\varepsilon)$ and $N\delta\ge C\ln(D/\varepsilon)$ with $C$ absolute. We keep the explicit values for definiteness.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
The bound $\Theta(\ln(1/\varepsilon)/(1-\gamma))$ matches the exact deterministic optimum for span-class algorithms on contractions \cite{ParkRyu2022}, and is achieved \emph{$\gamma$-obliviously}: the schedule contains no $\gamma$. The mechanism is visible in the proof: the anchor injected at step $k$ carries mass $O\bigl(c\,k^{c-1}N^{-c}\bigr)$ and is attenuated by $\gamma^{N-1-k}$; summed against the geometric envelope the total anchor contribution is $O(Dc)=O(\varepsilon)$, while the unanchored part contracts geometrically to $\gamma^ND\le\varepsilon/2$. (Keeping the kernel is essential: dropping it would replace the bound by $c\sum_k\gamma^{N-1-k}/(k+2)$, whose value grows like $e^{-N\delta}\ln(1/\delta)$ along the boundary $N\delta=4\ln(2D/\varepsilon)$ and is not uniformly $O(1)$; it is precisely the kernel factor $(k/N)^c$ that keeps the early anchors under control.) The same mechanism underlies the geometric batch schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch}.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:kernel}
For the schedule \eqref{eq:ck-schedule}, $c\in(0,1]$, there are absolute constants $c_1,c_2>0$ such that for all $1\le k\le n-1$ and $n\ge n_0(c)$,
$$c_1\,\frac{c}{k+1}\Bigl(\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)^{c} \;\le\; a_k^{(n)} \;\le\; c_2\,\frac{c}{k+1}\Bigl(\frac{k}{n}\Bigr)^{c}, \qquad e^{-2}(n+1)^{-c} \;\le\; r_n \;\le\; e^{1-\gamma_E}(n+1)^{-c} \;\le\; 2(n+1)^{-c},$$
where $\gamma_E$ is the Euler--Mascheroni constant. Moreover $\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)} = 1 - r_n$ and $\sum_{k=0}^{n-1} k\,a_k^{(n)} \le c_2 c\, n$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
For $j\ge 0$, $\theta_j=c/(j+2)\le 1/2$ and $\theta_j\le-\ln(1-\theta_j)\le\theta_j+\theta_j^2$ with $\sum_j\theta_j^2\le c^2(\pi^2/6-1)\le1$, so $\prod_{j=k+1}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j) = \exp(-\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\theta_j + O(1))$ and $\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\frac{c}{j+2} = c\ln\frac{n}{k} + O(1)$ (the shift by $2$ in the harmonic sum is absorbed into $O(1)$, uniformly for $k\ge1$); combining gives the two-sided bound on $a_k^{(n)}$. For $r_n$: $\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\theta_j=c(H_{n+1}-1)$ with $H_m=\ln m+\gamma_E+O(1/m)$, hence
$$r_n=\exp\bigl(-c\ln(n+1)-c(\gamma_E-1)+O(1)\bigr),\qquad |O(1)|\le \sum_j\theta_j^2\le 1,$$
which gives the display: the lower bound from $-\ln(1-\theta_j)\le\theta_j+\theta_j^2$ and $H_{n+1}-1\ge\ln(n+1)-(1-\gamma_E)$, the upper from $-\ln(1-\theta_j)\ge\theta_j$ and $H_{n+1}-1\le\ln(n+1)$ (since $\gamma_E+1/(2(n+1))\le1$ for $n\ge1$). The sum identities follow from telescoping $\theta_k\prod_{j=k+1}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j) = P_{k+1}-P_k$ with $P_k:=\prod_{j=k}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j)$: $\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)} = P_n - P_0 = 1 - r_n$ (with $P_n:=1$); and $\sum_k k a_k^{(n)}\le c_2 c n^{-c}\sum_k k^{c} \le c_2 c n$ for $c\in(0,1]$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:rotation-floor}
For every $n\ge n_0(c)$ and every $\varphi\le \tfrac{1}{4c_2 c n}$ (with $c_2$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}),
$$\norm{x_n} \;\ge\; \tfrac12 \qquad \text{on the instance } (R_\varphi, x_0).$$
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Identify $\mathcal{H}\cong\mathbb{C}$. By \eqref{eq:rot-unfold}, $y_n = e^{-i\varphi}A_n(\varphi) + r_n e^{-in\varphi}$ with $A_n(\varphi):=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)}e^{ik\varphi}$, so $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}\ge |A_n(\varphi)| - r_n$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}, $A_n(0) = 1-r_n\ge 3/4$ for $n\ge n_0(c)$, and
$$|A_n(\varphi)-A_n(0)| \;\le\; \varphi\sum_k k a_k^{(n)} \;\le\; c_2 c n\varphi \;\le\; \tfrac14,$$
hence $|A_n(\varphi)|\ge (1-r_n)-\tfrac14$, and
$$\norm{x_n}\;\ge\;\bigl|A_n(\varphi)\bigr|-r_n\;\ge\;\tfrac34-2r_n\;\ge\;\tfrac12\qquad\text{for }n\ge n_0(c)\text{ with }r_n\le\tfrac18$$
(enlarging $n_0(c)$ if necessary so that $r_n\le 2(n+1)^{-c}\le\tfrac18$).
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:rotation-law}
Let $A_n(\varphi):=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)}e^{ik\varphi}$ and $r_n:=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j)$, so that $y_n=e^{-i\varphi}A_n(\varphi)+r_ne^{-in\varphi}$ and $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}$ (display~\eqref{eq:rot-unfold}).

{\rm(i)} (Fixed $\varphi\in(0,\pi]$.) For $c\in(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rot-fixed}
A_n(\varphi)\;=\;c\,n^{-c}\,\operatorname{Li}_{1-c}\bigl(e^{i\varphi}\bigr)\;+\;O_c\bigl(n^{-c}\bigr)\qquad(n\to\infty).
\end{equation}
For $c=1$ one has the exact formula
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rot-exact}
A_n(\varphi)\;=\;\frac{1}{n+1}\cdot\frac{1-e^{in\varphi}}{1-e^{i\varphi}},
\end{equation}
so $nA_n(\varphi)$ has no limit as $n\to\infty$ at fixed $\varphi$; the two regimes must be separated.

{\rm(ii)} (Joint regime $n\to\infty$, $\varphi=\varphi(n)\to0^+$ with $n\varphi\to\infty$.) For $c\in(0,1)$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rot-joint}
A_n(\varphi)\;=\;\Gamma(c+1)\,(-in\varphi)^{-c}\Bigl(1+O_c(\varphi^{c})+O_c\bigl((n\varphi)^{c-1}\bigr)\Bigr),
\end{equation}
using the Jonqui\`ere expansion $\operatorname{Li}_{1-c}(e^{i\varphi})=\Gamma(c)(-i\varphi)^{-c}+\zeta(1-c)+O(\varphi^{1-c})$ as $\varphi\to0^+$, with $c\,\zeta(1-c)$ bounded on $(0,1)$. For $c=1$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rot-joint-one}
\norm{x_n}\;=\;\frac{2|\sin(n\varphi/2)|}{n\varphi}\;+\;O\Bigl(\frac1n\Bigr)\qquad\text{uniformly in } n,
\end{equation}
so the trajectory oscillates within the envelope $\asymp(n\varphi)^{-1}$ with additive slack $O(1/n)$; on the phases $n\varphi\in2\pi\mathbb Z$ one has $\norm{x_n}=(1+o(1))\,r_n=(1+o(1))/(n+1)$, and, more generally, $\norm{x_n}\le(2+o(1))(n\varphi)^{-1}$ and $\norm{x_n}\ge c\,|\sin(n\varphi/2)|/(n\varphi)-O(1/n)$. Consequently the two-sided law $\norm{x_n}\asymp\Gamma(c+1)(n\varphi)^{-c}$ holds for $c\in(0,1)$, while for $c=1$ only the upper bound and the lower bound on phases complementary to the zeros are uniform.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
\emph{Kernel asymptotic.} For $1\le k\le n-1$, since $\theta_j\le1/2$, $\theta_j\le-\ln(1-\theta_j)\le\theta_j+\theta_j^2$, hence $\prod_{j=k+1}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j)=\exp\big(-\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\theta_j-R_k\big)$ with $0\le R_k\le\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\theta_j^2\le c^2/(k+1)$. By Euler--Maclaurin ($H_m=\ln m+\gamma_E+O(1/m)$),
$$\sum_{j=k+1}^{n-1}\theta_j=c\,(H_{n+1}-H_{k+2})=c\ln\frac{n}{k}+O\Big(\frac{c}{k}\Big),$$
uniformly for $1\le k\le n-1$ (the $O(1/n)$ terms are absorbed into $O(1/k)$). Since also $\theta_k=\frac{c}{k}(1+O(\frac1k))$, multiplying gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kernel-pointwise}
a_k^{(n)}=c\,n^{-c}k^{c-1}\Big(1+O\Big(\frac1k\Big)\Big)\qquad\text{uniformly for }1\le k\le n-1,
\end{equation}
with a constant depending only on $c$.

\emph{Proof of {\rm(i)}.} Using \eqref{eq:kernel-pointwise},
$$A_n(\varphi)\;=\;c\,n^{-c}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{c-1}e^{ik\varphi}\;+\;O\Big(c\,n^{-c}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{c-2}\Big)\;=\;c\,n^{-c}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{c-1}e^{ik\varphi}\;+\;O_c(n^{-c}),$$
because $\sum_{k\ge1}k^{c-2}$ converges for $c\in(0,1)$ and $a_0^{(n)}=\Theta(r_n)=O(n^{-c})$. Since $k^{c-1}$ is decreasing in $k$, Abel summation against the geometric series gives
$$\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{c-1}e^{ik\varphi}\;=\;\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{c-1}e^{ik\varphi}\;-\;\sum_{k\ge n}k^{c-1}e^{ik\varphi}\;=\;\operatorname{Li}_{1-c}(e^{i\varphi})\;-\;R_n,$$
with $\abs{R_n}\le n^{c-1}/\sin(\varphi/2)=O_c(n^{c-1})$ for fixed $\varphi$ (Abel's inequality), so the tail contributes $O_c(n^{-1})$, which is absorbed into $O_c(n^{-c})$. This proves \eqref{eq:rot-fixed}; the error order $n^{-c}$ is sharp, owing to the $k=0$ term. For $c=1$ one has $a_k^{(n)}=r_n=\frac1{n+1}$ for all $k$, and \eqref{eq:rot-exact} follows by summing the geometric series; the non-existence of the limit is then immediate.

\emph{Proof of {\rm(ii)}.} Let $c\in(0,1)$. The main term comes from the Jonqui\`ere expansion with $c\,\Gamma(c)=\Gamma(c+1)$. The $\zeta(1-c)$ term contributes $c\,n^{-c}\zeta(1-c)$, a factor $\varphi^{c}$ below the main term since $c\,\zeta(1-c)$ stays bounded as $c\to0$ ($\zeta$ having a simple pole at $1$). The kernel correction contributes $O_c(n^{-c})$, also a factor $\varphi^c$ below the main term. The Abel tail contributes
$$c\,n^{-c}\abs{R_n}\;\le\;c\,n^{-c}\cdot\frac{n^{c-1}}{\sin(\varphi/2)}\;=\;O\Big(\frac{c}{n\varphi}\Big),$$
a factor $(n\varphi)^{c-1}$ below the main term $\Gamma(c+1)(n\varphi)^{-c}$. This proves \eqref{eq:rot-joint}. For $c=1$, \eqref{eq:rot-exact} gives
$$A_n(\varphi)\;=\;\frac{1-e^{in\varphi}}{n+1}\Big(\frac{i}{\varphi}+\frac12+O(\varphi)\Bigr)\;=\;\frac{i(1-e^{in\varphi})}{n\varphi}\Big(1+O(\varphi)+O\bigl((n\varphi)^{-1}\bigr)\Bigr),$$
using $(1-e^{i\varphi})^{-1}=i/\varphi+1/2+O(\varphi)$. Finally, since $|A_n(\varphi)|=\frac{2|\sin(n\varphi/2)|}{n\varphi}\bigl(1+O(\varphi)+O((n\varphi)^{-1})\bigr)$, $|A_n(\varphi)|\varphi=O(1/n)$, and $r_n=O(1/n)$,
$$\bigl|\norm{x_n}-|A_n(\varphi)|\bigr|\;\le\;|A_n(\varphi)|\,|e^{-i\varphi}-1|+r_n\;=\;O\Big(\frac1n\Bigr),$$
which proves \eqref{eq:rot-joint-one}. The remaining statements are immediate.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:envelope}
There are constants $c_4,\varphi_0(c),c_7>0$ depending only on $c$ (with $c_4<2\pi$ when $c=1$; $\varphi_0(c)\le c_7^{1/c}$) such that for all $0<\varphi\le\varphi_0(c)$ and all $n$ with $n_0(c)\le n\le c_4/\varphi$,
$$\norm{x_n}\;\ge\;c_7\,\min\bigl\{1,\,(n\varphi)^{-c}\bigr\}.$$
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Set $a_{0}:=(4c_2c)^{-1}$ (with $c_2$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel}).

\emph{Zero-free interval for $F_c$.} Write $F_c(a)=\int_0^1 t^{c-1}e^{iat}\,dt$. Integration by parts gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Fc-parts}
cF_c(a)\;=\;e^{ia}\;-\;ia\,F_{c+1}(a),\qquad |F_{c+1}(a)|\le\int_0^1 t^{c}\,dt=\frac1{c+1}\le1,
\end{equation}
so $|cF_c(a)|\ge 1-a$ for all $a>0$ and all $c\in(0,1]$; in particular $F_c$ has no zeros on $(0,1]$. Moreover $cF_c(a)=e^{ia}-iaF_{c+1}(a)$ and
$$\bigl|F_{c+1}(a)-\tfrac{e^{ia}-1}{ia}\bigr|\;=\;\Bigl|\int_0^1(t^{c}-1)e^{iat}\,dt\Bigr|\;\le\;\int_0^1(1-t^{c})\,dt\;=\;\frac{c}{c+1}\;\le\;c\qquad\text{for every }a,$$
so $F_{c+1}(a)\to(e^{ia}-1)/(ia)$ uniformly in $a$ as $c\to0$, and by \eqref{eq:Fc-parts} $cF_c(a)\to e^{ia}-(e^{ia}-1)=1$, uniformly for $a$ in compact sets. Hence there exist $c_\star\in(0,1)$ and $c_4'=c_4'(c_\star)\in(0,1)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Fc-lower}
|cF_c(a)|\;\ge\;\tfrac12\quad\text{for all }a\in(0,c_4'],\quad\text{all }c\in(0,c_\star),
\end{equation}
and for $c\ge c_\star$ we may enlarge the absolute constant $c_2$ (hence shrink $a_{0}=(4c_2c)^{-1}$) so that $a_{0}\le c_4'/2$. With this convention, \eqref{eq:Fc-lower} holds on $(a_{0},c_4']$ for every $c\in(0,1]$.

\emph{Range 1: $n\varphi\le a_{0}$.} Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation-floor} gives $\norm{x_n}\ge1/2$. If $n\varphi\le1$ the required bound with any $c_7\le1/2$ follows from $\min\{1,(n\varphi)^{-c}\}=1$; if $1\le n\varphi\le a_{0}$ (possible only when $a_{0}>1$) then $\min\{1,(n\varphi)^{-c}\}=(n\varphi)^{-c}\ge a_{0}^{-c}=(4c_2c)^{c}$, and the requirement is $c_7(4c_2c)^{c}\le1/2$. Hence Range 1 is covered provided
$$c_7\;\le\;\tfrac12\min\bigl\{1,\,(4c_2c)^{-c}\bigr\}.$$

\emph{Range 2: $a_{0}\le n\varphi\le c_4$.} Write $n=a/\varphi$ with $a\in[a_{0},c_4]$, where $c_4=c_4(c)\le c_4'$, with $c_4<2\pi$ when $c=1$ (then $cF_1(a)=(1-e^{ia})/(ia)$ has $|cF_1(a)|=2|\sin(a/2)|/a$). By \eqref{eq:kernel-pointwise} and dominated convergence (the $O(1/k)$ kernel correction and the $k=O(1)$ boundary layer each contribute $O(n^{-c})$),
$$A_n(\varphi)\;=\;c\,n^{-c}\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}k^{c-1}e^{i(a/n)k}\bigl(1+O(\tfrac1k)\bigr)+a_0^{(n)}\;\longrightarrow\;cF_c(a)\;:=\;c\int_0^1t^{c-1}e^{iat}\,dt,$$
uniformly for $a\in[a_{0},c_4]$ as $\varphi\to0$. Define $\mu_c:=\min_{a\in[a_{0},c_4]}|cF_c(a)|$: by \eqref{eq:Fc-lower} and the choice of $c_4<2\pi$ for $c=1$, we have $\mu_c>0$. For $\varphi$ small enough (depending only on $c$) the convergence above gives $\abs{A_n(\varphi)}\ge\mu_c/2$, while $r_n\le2(n+1)^{-c}=O(\varphi^{c})$; shrinking $\varphi_0(c)$ further we ensure $r_n\le\mu_c/4$, hence
$$\norm{x_n}\;\ge\;\abs{A_n(\varphi)}-r_n\;\ge\;\tfrac14\abs{cF_c(a)}.$$

\emph{Choice of constants.} Define
$$c_7\;:=\;\tfrac14\min\Bigl\{\mu_c,\ \inf_{a\in[a_{0}\vee1,\,c_4]}\abs{cF_c(a)}\,a^{c},\ (4c_2c)^{-c}\Bigr\}\quad(<\tfrac12),$$
which satisfies the Range-1 requirement above. For $a\in[a_{0},1]$: $\norm{x_n}\ge\mu_c/4\ge c_7=c_7\min\{1,a^{-c}\}$. For $a\in[1,c_4]$: $\norm{x_n}\ge\abs{cF_c(a)}/4\ge c_7a^{-c}$ by the second entry of the minimum. Finally fix $\varphi_0(c)>0$ small enough that the convergence above is uniform, $r_n\le\mu_c/4$ throughout Range 2, and $\varphi_0(c)\le c_7^{1/c}$.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
Lemma~\ref{lem:rotation-law} explains the numerics precisely: for fixed small $\varphi$ and $c<1$ the log--log slope of $\norm{x_n}$ versus $n$ is $-c$ (Section~\ref{sec:numerics}, Experiment~3); for $c=1$ the trajectory follows the exact envelope $\norm{x_n}=\frac{2|\sin(n\varphi/2)|}{n\varphi}+O(1/n)$, oscillating between $\sim2/(n\varphi)$ and $\sim1/n$ (the dips occur at $n\varphi\in2\pi\mathbb Z$, where the trajectory takes the value $(1+o(1))/(n+1)=r_n$ rather than $0$). For $c<1$ the decay is \emph{slower} algebraically, and as $c\to0$ the prefactor $\Gamma(c+1)\to1$ while the exponent $-c\to0$: the rotation is barely damped. The super-polynomial cost for contraction-optimal schedules comes from solving $\Gamma(c+1)(n\varphi)^{-c}\le\varepsilon$.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:separation}
Fix $\varepsilon<\min\{1/8,D/4\}$, additionally small in terms of $c$ (quantified in the proof of {\rm(ii)}), and a schedule \eqref{eq:ck-schedule} with constant $c\in(0,1]$. Let $N_{\rho}(\varepsilon)$ be the worst-case hitting time on the $\rho$-contraction class and $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)$ the hitting time on the rotation $R_\varphi$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item If $c=1$ (the classical Halpern schedule $\theta_n=1/(n+2)$), then for every $\rho\in(0,1)$,
$$N_\rho(\varepsilon) \;\ge\; \frac{D(1-2\varepsilon)}{2(1-\rho)\varepsilon}.$$
\item If $c\le \varepsilon/(96D)$, then for every $\rho\in(0,1)$, $N_\rho(\varepsilon)\le 4\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\rho))/(1-\rho)+1$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c}; but there is a rotation angle $\varphi=\varphi(\varepsilon,c)>0$ with
$$N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\varepsilon^{-1/c},$$
and in particular, since $c=O(\varepsilon/D)$ forces $\varepsilon^{-1/c}=\exp(\Omega(\tfrac{D}{\varepsilon}\ln\tfrac1\varepsilon))$, no member of the family is simultaneously contraction-optimal and rotation-polynomial: adaptivity is necessary on the union class within this family, quantitatively.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
(i) On the scalar instance $T=\rho\,\mathrm{id}$, $x_0=D$, the error satisfies $e_{n+1}=\frac{D}{n+2}+\frac{n+1}{n+2}\rho e_n$; induction gives the closed form $e_N=\frac{D(1-\rho^{N+1})}{(1-\rho)(N+1)}$ (telescoping product $\prod_{j=0}^{N-1}\frac{j+1}{j+2}=\frac{1}{N+1}$, with $e_0=D$). Hence $e_N\le\varepsilon$ forces $N+1\ge\frac{D(1-\rho^{N+1})}{(1-\rho)\varepsilon}\ge\frac{D(1-2\varepsilon)}{(1-\rho)\varepsilon}$ once $N\ge\ln(1/(2\varepsilon))/(1-\rho)$ (which guarantees $\rho^{N+1}\le2\varepsilon$), and in particular $N\ge\frac{D(1-2\varepsilon)}{2(1-\rho)\varepsilon}$ whenever the right-hand side exceeds $1$ (otherwise the bound is vacuous).

(ii) The contraction claim is Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c}. For the rotation claim set $\varphi=\min\{\varphi_0(c),\,\frac{c_4}{2}(c_7\varepsilon)^{1/c}\}$ with $\varphi_0,c_4,c_7$ from Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope}. The scales $n\le n_0(c)$ not covered by Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope} are handled by the universal rotation floor, valid for every schedule: $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}\ge\Re(e^{in\varphi}y_n)=\sum_{k<n}a_k^{(n)}\cos((n-k-1)\varphi)+r_n\ge\tfrac12(1-r_n)+r_n\ge\tfrac12$ whenever $(n-1)\varphi\le\pi/3$, which holds for all $n\le n_0(c)$ as soon as $\varepsilon$ is small enough that $\frac{c_4}{2}(c_7\varepsilon)^{1/c}\le\frac{\pi}{3(n_0(c)-1)}$ (recall $\varphi\le\frac{c_4}{2}(c_7\varepsilon)^{1/c}\to0$). For $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small relative to $D$ this satisfies $\varphi\le\varphi_0(c)$ and $(c_7/\varepsilon)^{1/c}\le c_4/\varphi$ (the latter because $c_4/\varphi\ge 2(c_7\varepsilon)^{-1/c}\ge (c_7/\varepsilon)^{1/c}$, using $c_7\le 2^{-c/2}$, which holds since $c_7<1/2$). By Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope}, $\norm{x_n}\ge c_7(n\varphi)^{-c}$ for all $n\le c_4/\varphi$, so $\norm{x_n}\le\varepsilon$ requires
$$n\;\ge\;\Bigl(\frac{c_7}{\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{1/c}\varphi^{-1}\;\ge\;c_7^{1/c}\varphi_0(c)^{-1}\,\varepsilon^{-1/c}\;\ge\;\varepsilon^{-1/c},$$
where the last inequality uses $\varphi_0(c)\le c_7^{1/c}$.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:adaptive-escape}
Theorem~\ref{thm:separation} is the \emph{necessity} half of the adaptivity story: within the family \eqref{eq:ck-schedule}, no fixed shape escapes. The \emph{sufficiency} half has three layers, all proved or cited in Section~\ref{sec:adaptive-escape}: (i) an explicit \emph{parallel two-track scheme} (Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel}) that is simultaneously optimal on both classes --- $O(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/(1-\gamma))$ on contractions and $O(D/\varepsilon)$ on nonexpansive maps --- giving the sharp converse to Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general} at factor-$2$ cost; (ii) a robustness theorem (Theorem~\ref{thm:doubling-window}): adaptive window restarts, the natural single-trajectory escape, improve the classical contraction time only by a constant factor --- the barrier is robust against this adaptive class; and (iii) the structurally adaptive parameter-free methods \cite{ParameterFree2026,Diakonikolas2025}, which are stochastic-relevant. What was not known, and what this section supplies, is the quantitative mechanism --- the rotation Fourier spectrum $\varphi\mapsto\sum_k a_k^{(n)}e^{ik\varphi}$ of the schedule --- the sharpness of the tradeoff (contraction-optimality forces $c=\Theta(\varepsilon)$, which forces $\varepsilon^{-1/c}$ on rotations), and the detection barrier (Remark~\ref{rem:detection}) explaining why single-trajectory optimality cannot be reached by trajectory statistics.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:anchor-floor}
On the scalar instance $T=\rho\,\mathrm{id}$, $x_0=D$, the error of \eqref{eq:anchored} satisfies the exact identity
\begin{equation}\label{eq:anchor-floor}
e_N \;=\; D\Bigl[\rho^N r_N \;+\; \sum_{k=0}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}\rho^{N-1-k}\Bigr]
\;\ge\; D\,\rho^{t}\sum_{k=N-t}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}
\qquad\text{for every }t\in\{1,\dots,N\}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Unfolding $e_{n+1}=\theta_nD+(1-\theta_n)\rho e_n$ (exact on this instance, all terms nonnegative) gives the identity; the inequality drops the terms $k<N-t$ and uses $\rho^{N-1-k}\ge\rho^t$ for $k\ge N-t$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:rot-floor}
Identify $\mathcal{H}\cong\mathbb{C}$, $x_0=1$. For every schedule and every rotation angle $\varphi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rot-floor}
\norm{x_n} \;\ge\; \sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)}\cos\bigl((n-k-1)\varphi\bigr) \;+\; r_n
\;\ge\; \tfrac12
\end{equation}
whenever $(n-1)\varphi\le\tfrac{\pi}{3}$ (in particular, whenever $n\varphi\le\tfrac{\pi}{3}$).
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
From display~\eqref{eq:rot-unfold}, 
$$y_n=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k^{(n)}e^{-i(k+1)\varphi}+r_ne^{-in\varphi},$$
so $\Re(e^{in\varphi}y_n)=\sum_ka_k^{(n)}\cos((n-k-1)\varphi)+r_n$, and $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}\ge\Re(e^{in\varphi}y_n)$. If $(n-1)\varphi\le\pi/3$ then $(n-k-1)\varphi\in[0,\pi/3]$ for all $k\le n-1$, so each cosine is at least $\tfrac12$ and the sum is at least $\tfrac12(1-r_n)$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
\label{rem:why-floor-matters}
Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor-floor} replaces the kernel asymptotics of Lemma~\ref{lem:kernel} on the contraction side (the window mass $\sum_{k\ge N-t}a_k^{(N)}$ is controlled directly), and Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor} replaces the Fourier analysis on the rotation side: it shows that a rotation slower than the schedule's resolution scale ($n\varphi=O(1)$) is never solved: the anchors keep pulling toward directions that have not yet wound apart.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:dichotomy-general}
Let $\theta_n=\frac{c_n}{n+2}$ with $c_n\in(0,1]$ and let $M(n)=\sum_{j<n}\theta_j$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item If $\liminf_{n\to\infty}c_n\ge c_0>0$, then for every $\rho\in(0,1)$, all $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:general-contract-lb}
N_\rho(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\Bigl(\frac{c_0\,\rho\,D}{16e^{3}\,\varepsilon(1-\rho)}\Bigr)^{1/3},
\end{equation}
i.e.\ the schedule is polynomially suboptimal on contractions, by a factor $\Omega\bigl((\rho D/(\varepsilon(1-\rho)))^{1/3}(1-\rho)/\ln(D/\varepsilon)\bigr)$.
\item If $c_n\to0$, then either
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item $\sum_n\theta_n<\infty$: there is $\varphi_0>0$ such that $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)=\infty$ for all $\varphi\le\varphi_0$ and $\varepsilon<\tfrac14$ (the schedule never solves small-angle rotations); or
\item $\sum_n\theta_n=\infty$: for every $B\ge1$ and every $\varepsilon>0$ sufficiently small there is an angle $\varphi_B(\varepsilon)>0$, with $\varphi_B(\varepsilon)\to0$ as $\varepsilon\to0$, such that $N_{\varphi_B(\varepsilon)}(\varepsilon)>\varepsilon^{-B}$ --- super-polynomially slow on rotations.
\end{enumerate}
\end{enumerate}
In particular every anchor schedule with $c_n\in(0,1]$ is either polynomially suboptimal on contractions or super-polynomially (possibly infinitely) slow on rotations; the family $\theta_n=c/(n+2)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:separation} is the constant-density case, where the exponents are sharp. The remaining case $\liminf c_n=0<\limsup c_n$ is resolved in Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} below: the dichotomy fails there, and the boundary is exactly the lacunarity of the dense anchor scales (Remark~\ref{rem:oscillatory}, Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-bounded-gap}).
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
(i) Since $\theta_j\le\tfrac12$, $\abs{\ln r_n+M(n)}\le\sum_j\theta_j^2\le M(n)$ and $c_j\le1$ gives $M(n)\le\ln(n+2)$; hence
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rn-general}
r_n\;\ge\;(n+2)^{-2}\;\ge\;(2n)^{-2}\qquad(n\ge2).
\end{equation}
Choose $n_1$ with $c_k\ge c_0/2$ for $k\ge n_1$ and let $t=\lceil 1/(1-\rho)\rceil$, $N\ge\max(2n_1,2t)$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:window-mass}
\sum_{k=N-t}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}\;\ge\;r_N\sum_{k=N-t}^{N-1}\theta_k\;\ge\;r_N\cdot\frac{c_0}{2}\cdot\frac{t}{N+2}\;\ge\;\frac{c_0\,r_N}{4N(1-\rho)} .
\end{equation}
If $\rho\ge\tfrac12$, then $t(1-\rho)\le2$ and $\rho^t\ge e^{-3}$ (since $\ln\rho\ge-2(1-\rho)$ for $\rho\ge\tfrac12$ and $t(1-\rho)\le1+\delta\le\tfrac32$), so Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor-floor} and \eqref{eq:rn-general} give
$$e_N\;\ge\;\frac{De^{-3}c_0r_N}{4N(1-\rho)}\;\ge\;\frac{c_0\rho D}{16e^{3}N^{3}(1-\rho)},$$
using $r_N\ge(2N)^{-2}\ge\rho/(4N^{2})$; $e_N\le\varepsilon$ forces \eqref{eq:general-contract-lb}. If $\rho<\tfrac12$, Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor-floor} with $t=1$ gives $e_N\ge D\rho\,\theta_{N-1}\ge D\rho c_0/(2(N+1))\ge c_0\rho D/(6N)$, which dominates the cube-root display whenever the latter is $\ge1$ (i.e.\ whenever $c_0\rho D/(6\varepsilon)\ge(c_0\rho D/(16e^3\varepsilon(1-\rho)))^{1/3}$, which holds for all sufficiently small $\varepsilon$ since the left side grows like $1/\varepsilon$ and the right like $1/\varepsilon^{1/3}$), and is otherwise vacuous.

(ii) (a) As in Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}, $\norm{x_{n+1}}\ge(1-\theta_n)\norm{x_n}-\theta_n$ gives $\norm{x_n}\ge\norm{x_{n_0}}-2\sum_{j\ge n_0}\theta_j$; by continuity at $\varphi=0$ (where the orbit is constant) pick $\varphi_1$ and $n_0$ with $\norm{x_{n_0}}\ge3/4$ and $2\sum_{j\ge n_0}\theta_j\le1/4$: then $\norm{x_n}\ge1/2$ for all $n\ge n_0$, for every $\varphi\le\varphi_1$; shrinking $\varphi_1$ further so that $\varphi_1\le\pi/(3n_0)$, Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor} gives $\norm{x_n}\ge1/2$ for the remaining $n<n_0$ as well, hence for all $n$ and every $\varphi\le\varphi_1$.

(ii) (b) We first show $M(n)=o(\ln n)$. For any $\delta>0$, $M(n)\le M(n^{1-\delta})+\bigl(\max_{n^{1-\delta}\le j<n}c_j\bigr)\ln n^{\delta}$, and $c_j\to0$ makes the max $o(1)$; with $\delta=\tfrac12$ this gives $\limsup M(n)/\ln n\le\tfrac12\limsup M(\sqrt n)/\ln(\sqrt n)$, hence the limsup is $0$. Since $\sum_n\theta_n=\infty$, $M(n)\to\infty$. Fix $B\ge1$. Since $M(n)=o(\ln n)$ there is $n_0'$ with $M(n)\le\ln n$ for all $n\ge n_0'$. For $\varepsilon$ small let $n_B=n_B(\varepsilon)$ be the first integer with $M(n_B)\ge B\ln(1/\varepsilon)$ (so $n_B\to\infty$ as $\varepsilon\to0$); for $\varepsilon$ small enough that $n_B\ge n_0'$ (this is the only point at which the smallness of $\varepsilon$ enters),
$$B\ln(1/\varepsilon)\;\le\;M(n_B)\;\le\;\ln n_B,\qquad\text{i.e.}\qquad n_B\;\ge\;\varepsilon^{-B}.$$
Set $\varphi_B(\varepsilon)=\pi/(3n_B)$: then $\varphi_B(\varepsilon)\to0$ as $\varepsilon\to0$, and for every $n\le n_B$, $n\varphi_B(\varepsilon)\le\pi/3$, so Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor} gives $\norm{x_n}\ge1/2>\varepsilon$: $N_{\varphi_B(\varepsilon)}(\varepsilon)>n_B\ge\varepsilon^{-B}$.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:oscillatory-escape}
Fix $\varepsilon\le D/4$ and set $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)$. Define the schedule
$$\theta_n\;=\;\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{n+2}, & n\in B_j:=\{2^{j},\dots,3\cdot 2^{j-1}-1\}\ \text{for some }j\ge1,\\[1.2ex]\dfrac{c'}{n+2}, & n\in S_j:=\{3\cdot 2^{j-1},\dots,2^{j+1}-1\}\ \text{for some }j\ge1.\end{cases}$$
(The blocks $B_j$ have length $2^{j-1}$ and carry the classical anchor mass; the stretches $S_j$ have length $2^{j-1}$ and carry mass $c'$. The indices $n\in\{0,1\}$ lie in no $B_j$ or $S_j$; set $\theta_n=1/(n+2)$ there --- the choice is immaterial to every rate below. The schedule is fixed and $\gamma$-oblivious.) Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Contraction regime.} For every $\gamma$-contraction, writing $\delta=1-\gamma$,
$$\norm{x_n-x^*}\;\le\;\tfrac{3\varepsilon}{4}\qquad\text{for some } n=O\Bigl(\frac{\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\delta}\Bigr),$$
matching the $\gamma$-aware deterministic optimum up to a universal constant.
\item \emph{Rotation regime.} For every rotation angle $\varphi\in(0,1]$, writing $\alpha_0=\log_2(3/2)$,
$$\norm{x_n}\;\le\;\varepsilon\qquad\text{for some } n=O\Bigl(\varphi^{-1}\,\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0}\Bigr),$$
(the implicit constant may depend on $D=\norm{x_0}$, both through $c'$ and through the overall factor $\norm{x_0}$ in the rotating-frame unfolding; rotations have $x^*=0$)
polynomial in $1/\varepsilon$ with the lacunarity exponent $1/\alpha_0\approx1.71$ --- far below the super-polynomial barrier $\varepsilon^{-1/c}$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(ii). The matching lower bound $\Omega(\varphi^{-1})$ is proved (Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor}), and the matching lower bound is given, at each fixed accuracy, for Lebesgue-a.e.\ angle in Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(i) below.
\end{enumerate}
Hence the dichotomy of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general} does \emph{not} extend to oscillatory densities: Remark~\ref{rem:oscillatory}'s conjecture is false. The boundary is sharp: dense anchor scales with \emph{bounded gaps} force polynomial contraction time (Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-bounded-gap}), while \emph{lacunary} dense scales --- as here, where $\{n:\theta_n\ge c''\}$ has gaps $2^{j-1}\to\infty$ for every $c''\in(c',1)$ --- admit a fixed schedule optimal on both regimes (on the rotation side, up to the constant in the metric lacunarity law, Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}). Adaptivity is necessary \emph{precisely when the dense anchor scales are non-lacunary}.
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Let $a_k^{(n)}=\theta_k\prod_{i=k+1}^{n-1}(1-\theta_i)$ and $r_n=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(1-\theta_i)$ as usual.

\emph{Proof of {\rm(i)}.} Let $s=3\cdot2^{j^\star-1}$ be the start of the stretch $S_{j^\star}$, where $j^\star$ is the smallest $j$ with $2^{j-1}\ge\ln(8D/\varepsilon)/\delta$, and stop at
$$n'\;=\;s+t',\qquad t'=\Bigl\lceil\frac{\ln(8D/\varepsilon)}{\delta}\Bigr\rceil ;$$
then $n'\le 6\ln(8D/\varepsilon)/\delta+\ln(8D/\varepsilon)/\delta+1=O\bigl(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta\bigr)$, since minimality gives $2^{j^\star-1}<2\ln(8D/\varepsilon)/\delta$. Throughout, $e_k:=\norm{x_k-x^*}\le D$ for every $k$, by induction from $e_{k+1}\le\theta_kD+(1-\theta_k)\gamma e_k$ and $\theta_k+(1-\theta_k)\gamma\le1$. Unfolding \eqref{eq:onestep} from $s$,
$$e_{n'}\;\le\;\gamma^{t'}e_s\;+\;D\sum_{k=s}^{n'-1}a_k^{(n')}\gamma^{n'-1-k}.$$
The first term is at most $D\gamma^{t'}\le\varepsilon/8$ by the choice of $t'$ (using $\ln(1/\gamma)\ge\delta$). For the second term, $a_k^{(n')}\le\theta_k=c'/(k+2)\le c'/s$ on $[s,n']$, and $\sum_{j\ge0}\gamma^j=1/\delta$, so
$$D\sum_{k=s}^{n'-1}a_k^{(n')}\gamma^{n'-1-k}\;\le\;\frac{Dc'}{s}\cdot\frac{1}{\delta}\;\le\;\frac{Dc'}{3\ln(8D/\varepsilon)}\;=\;\frac{\varepsilon}{288\,\ln(8D/\varepsilon)}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon}{288\,\ln 32},$$
using $s\delta\ge3\ln(8D/\varepsilon)$. All anchors injected before $s$ --- blocks and stretches alike --- are absorbed in the bound $e_s\le D$ and contracted by the factor $\gamma^{t'}\le\varepsilon/(8D)$. Hence
$$e_{n'}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon}{8}+\frac{\varepsilon}{288\ln 32}\;<\;\frac{3\varepsilon}{4}.$$
Every step of this argument is valid for every $\delta\in(0,1)$; no smallness condition on $\varepsilon$ or $\delta$ is required.

\emph{Proof of {\rm(ii)}.} Work in the de-rotated frame $y_n=R_\varphi^{-n}x_n$ of Section~\ref{sec:det} (the frame is an isometry, so $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}$). The exact unfolding \eqref{eq:rot-unfold} gives
$$y_n\;=\;\sum_{k<n}a_k^{(n)}u_k\;+\;r_nR_\varphi^{-n}x_0,\qquad u_k=R_\varphi^{-(k+1)}x_0,\qquad a_k^{(n)}=\theta_k\,\frac{r_n}{r_{k+1}}.$$

\emph{Step 1: anchor mass and the residue rate.} The mass increment over one dyadic scale $[2^j,2^{j+1})$ is
$$\sum_{k\in B_j}\frac{1}{k+2}+\sum_{k\in S_j}\frac{c'}{k+2}\;=\;\ln\frac32\,+\,c'\ln\frac43\,+\,O(2^{-j})\;=\;\alpha\ln 2\,+\,O(2^{-j}),\qquad\alpha:=\log_2\frac32+c'\log_2\frac43,$$
so $M(n):=\sum_{k<n}\theta_k=\alpha\ln n+O(1)$, and since $\sum_k\theta_k^2<\infty$,
$$r_n\;=\;\exp\bigl(-M(n)+O(1)\bigr)\;\asymp\;n^{-\alpha}.$$
Note $1/\alpha=1/\alpha_0+O(\varepsilon)$ with $\alpha_0=\log_2(3/2)$, because $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)$.

\emph{Step 2: kernel flatness on the dense scales.} On a classical block $B_i$ starting at $s_i=2^i$, $\theta_k=1/(k+2)$ gives $r_{k+1}=r_{s_i}(s_i+1)/(k+2)$, so $a_k^{(n)}=r_n/\bigl((s_i+1)r_{s_i}\bigr)=:W_i^{(n)}$ is constant on $B_i$, with $W_i^{(n)}\asymp r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}$ (uniformly in $i$, since $r_{s_i}\asymp s_i^{-\alpha}$). On a stretch $S_i$ starting at $t_i=3\cdot 2^{i-1}$, $r_{k+1}=r_{t_i}\bigl((t_i+1)/(k+2)\bigr)^{c'}$, so $a_k^{(n)}=c'r_n(k+2)^{c'-1}/\bigl((t_i+1)^{c'}r_{t_i}\bigr)$ varies by at most a factor $2$ on $S_i$, and $a_{t_i}^{(n)}\asymp c'r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}$.

\emph{Step 3: chord bounds.} For consecutive integers, $\bigl|\sum_{k=s}^{s+\ell-1}e^{-i(k+1)\varphi}\bigr|\le\min\{\ell,\,\pi/\varphi\}$ for $\varphi\in(0,1]$ (using $\sin(\varphi/2)\ge\varphi/\pi$). Split the dyadic pieces starting below $n$ into \emph{wide} pieces ($2^{i-1}\varphi\ge\pi$) and \emph{narrow} pieces ($2^{i-1}\varphi<\pi$). We make the weight bounds of Step 2 explicit: with the constants $c_0,C_0$ of Step 1 ($c_0j^{-\alpha}\le r_j\le C_0j^{-\alpha}$), every per-index weight on a piece at scale $i$ satisfies
$$a_k^{(n)}\;\le\;\frac{2}{c_0}\,r_n\,2^{i(\alpha-1)},$$
since on a block $W_i^{(n)}=r_n/((s_i+1)r_{s_i})\le r_n/(2^ic_02^{-i\alpha})=c_0^{-1}r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}$, and on a stretch $a_k^{(n)}=c'r_n(k+2)^{c'-1}/((t_i+1)^{c'}r_{t_i})\le2c'r_n/(t_ir_{t_i})\le(2c'/c_0)r_nt_i^{\alpha-1}\le(2/c_0)r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}$ (using $c'\le1$ and $t_i=3\cdot2^{i-1}\ge2^i$). Hence a piece at scale $i$ has mass at most $\frac{2}{c_0}r_n2^{i\alpha}$. Let $i_0$ be the smallest index with $2^{i_0-1}\varphi\ge\pi$; then $2^{i_0-2}<\pi/\varphi\le2^{i_0-1}$, i.e.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:wide-narrow}
\frac{2\pi}{\varphi}\;\le\;2^{i_0}\;<\;\frac{4\pi}{\varphi}.
\end{equation}
A wide piece contributes at most $(\max\text{weight})\cdot\pi/\varphi\le\frac{2\pi}{c_0\varphi}r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}$, and $\alpha<1$ gives the geometric sum
$$\sum_{\mathrm{wide}}\;\le\;\frac{2\pi}{c_0\varphi}\,r_n\sum_{i\ge i_0}2^{i(\alpha-1)}\;=\;\frac{2\pi}{c_0\varphi}\,r_n\cdot\frac{2^{i_0(\alpha-1)}}{1-2^{\alpha-1}}\;\le\;\frac{2\pi(2\pi)^{\alpha-1}}{c_0(1-2^{\alpha-1})}\,r_n\varphi^{-\alpha},$$
where the last step uses $2^{i_0(\alpha-1)}=(2^{i_0})^{\alpha-1}\le(2\pi/\varphi)^{\alpha-1}=(2\pi)^{\alpha-1}\varphi^{1-\alpha}$ (the power $\alpha-1$ is negative, so the lower bound in \eqref{eq:wide-narrow} is used). A narrow piece contributes at most its mass, so
$$\sum_{\mathrm{narrow}}\;\le\;\frac{2}{c_0}\,r_n\sum_{i<i_0}2^{i\alpha}\;=\;\frac{2}{c_0}\,r_n\cdot\frac{2^{i_0\alpha}-1}{2^\alpha-1}\;\le\;\frac{2(4\pi)^\alpha}{c_0(2^\alpha-1)}\,r_n\varphi^{-\alpha},$$
using $2^{i_0\alpha}<(4\pi/\varphi)^\alpha$ from \eqref{eq:wide-narrow} ($\alpha>0$). Since $\alpha\in[\alpha_0,3/5]$ with $\alpha_0=\log_2(3/2)$, both denominators are bounded below: $1-2^{\alpha-1}\ge1-2^{-2/5}>\tfrac15$ and $2^\alpha-1\ge\sqrt2-1>\tfrac25$, so both coefficients are universal multiples of $c_0^{-1}$.
The initial segment $k<2$ contributes at most $a_0^{(n)}+a_1^{(n)}\le2r_n$ (as $\theta_k\le1/(k+2)$ gives $a_k^{(n)}=\theta_kr_n/r_{k+1}\le r_n$ for $k=0,1$), and the top piece, possibly truncated at $m$, is covered by the same dichotomy: if it is wide, say at scale $2^i$, it contributes at most $\frac{2\pi}{c_0\varphi}r_n2^{i(\alpha-1)}=\frac{2\pi}{c_0}r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}(2^i\varphi)^{\alpha-1}\le\frac{2\pi(2\pi)^{\alpha-1}}{c_0}r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}$, since wideness gives $2^i\varphi\ge2\pi$ and $\alpha-1<0$; if it is narrow its mass is at most $\frac{2}{c_0}r_n2^{i\alpha}<\frac{2(2\pi)^\alpha}{c_0}r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}$, since then $2^i<2\pi/\varphi$. Hence, with $C_1$ universal (a fixed multiple of $c_0^{-1}$),
\begin{equation}\label{eq:osc-head}
\Bigl\|\sum_{k<m}a_k^{(n)}u_k\Bigr\|\;\le\;C_1\,r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}\qquad\text{for every }m\le n.
\end{equation}

\emph{Step 4: the fresh tail and hitting.} It suffices to prove the claim for $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$ ($\varepsilon_0\le1$ a constant to be fixed): $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)$ is nonincreasing in $\varepsilon$, so for $\varepsilon\ge\varepsilon_0$, $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\le N_\varphi(\varepsilon_0)\le C\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon_0^{-1/\alpha_0}\le C\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0}$. Fix $n$ with $n\varphi\ge8$ and $n\ge110/\varepsilon$, and split the sum at $m=n-w$ with $w=\lceil\alpha\varepsilon n/8\rceil$. Since $\alpha\le3/5$ (as $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)\le1/96$) and $1\le\varepsilon n/110$,
$$w\;\le\;\frac{\alpha\varepsilon n}{8}+1\;\le\;\frac{3\varepsilon n}{40}+\frac{\varepsilon n}{110}\;=\;\frac{37\varepsilon n}{440}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon n}{10},\qquad m=n-w\ge\frac{9n}{10}.$$
For the tail mass, $\theta_k/r_{k+1}=(r_k-r_{k+1})/(r_kr_{k+1})=1/r_{k+1}-1/r_k$, so the sum telescopes exactly:
$$\sum_{m\le k<n}a_k^{(n)}\;=\;r_n\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}\frac{\theta_k}{r_{k+1}}\;=\;r_n\Bigl(\frac1{r_n}-\frac1{r_m}\Bigr)\;=\;1-\frac{r_n}{r_m}.$$
By the Weierstrass product inequality $\prod_j(1-x_j)\ge1-\sum_jx_j$ (the two-factor form $(1-x)(1-y)\ge1-x-y$ iterated) and $\theta_k=c_k/(k+2)\le1/(k+2)\le1/m$ for $k\ge m$ (as $c_k\le1$),
$$1-\frac{r_n}{r_m}\;\le\;\sum_{k=m}^{n-1}\theta_k\;\le\;\frac{w}{m}\;\le\;\frac{37\varepsilon n}{440}\cdot\frac{10}{9n}\;=\;\frac{37\varepsilon}{396}\;<\;\frac{\varepsilon}{9}\;<\;\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.$$
The residue contributes $r_n\le C_0n^{-\alpha}\le C_0(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}$ (as $\varphi\le1$). Combining with \eqref{eq:osc-head}, and absorbing $\norm{x_0}=D$ into the universal constants,
$$\norm{x_n}\;\le\;C_1\,r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{9}+C_0(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}\;\le\;C_2(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}+\frac{\varepsilon}{9},$$
using $r_n\varphi^{-\alpha}\le C_0n^{-\alpha}\varphi^{-\alpha}=C_0(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}$. Choosing $n$ minimal with $n\varphi\ge\max\bigl\{8,\,(4C_2/\varepsilon)^{1/\alpha}\bigr\}$ and $n\ge110/\varepsilon$ gives $C_2(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}\le\varepsilon/4$, hence $\norm{x_n}\le(1/4+1/9)\varepsilon<\varepsilon/2<\varepsilon$; and $n=O\bigl(\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}\bigr)=O\bigl(\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0}\bigr)$, because the three lower bounds on $n$ are respectively $8\le8\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}$ (as $\varphi\le1$, $\varepsilon\le1$), $110/\varepsilon\le110\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}$ (as $\varepsilon^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}\le1\le\varphi^{-1}$), and $(4C_2/\varepsilon)^{1/\alpha}=(4C_2)^{1/\alpha}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}=O(\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0})$ for $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0(C_2)$ (since $1/\alpha=1/\alpha_0+O(\varepsilon)$ and $\varepsilon^{-O(\varepsilon)}=e^{O(\varepsilon\ln(1/\varepsilon))}=O(1)$).
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
\label{rem:osc-profile}
The schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} is simultaneously (a) \emph{optimal on $\mathcal{C}$}: $\Theta(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta)$, matching the Park--Ryu lower bound; (b) \emph{polynomial on rotations with the lacunarity exponent}: $O\bigl(\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0}\bigr)$, $\alpha_0=\log_2(3/2)$, $1/\alpha_0\approx1.71$ --- far below the super-polynomial barrier that every schedule with non-lacunary dense scales and contraction-optimal behavior must obey (Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(ii)), and, for Lebesgue-a.e.\ angle at each fixed accuracy, the matching lower bound (Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(i)), making the rate $\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0+o(1)}$ exact almost everywhere along a full-density set of accuracies; and (c) \emph{open on $\mathcal{N}$}: no worst-case residual guarantee on the full nonexpansive class is proved for this schedule. The naive expectation fails already on the rotation: $\norm{x_n-R_\varphi x_n}=2\sin(\varphi/2)\norm{x_n}\asymp\varphi\norm{x_n}$ for $\varphi\in(0,1]$, so applying Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}(ii) at accuracy $\varepsilon/(2\sin(\varphi/2))$ gives residual hitting time $O\bigl(\varphi^{(1-\alpha)/\alpha}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}\bigr)=O\bigl(\varphi^{(1-\alpha_0)/\alpha_0}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0+o(1)}\bigr)$ as $\varepsilon\to0$ at fixed $\varphi$ --- polynomial, but with exponent $1/\alpha_0\approx1.71>1$, not the optimal $\Theta(D/\varepsilon)$ that the classical schedule meets on $R_\varphi$ at $\varphi=\Theta(1)$; and the window minima track the envelope $(n\varphi)^{-\alpha_0}$ (Remark~\ref{rem:lacunarity-status}, Experiment~5), so on the evidence of that envelope the schedule is not residual-optimal on $\mathcal{N}$. Whether any single fixed stream is simultaneously contraction-optimal and residual-optimal on $\mathcal{N}$ is open. Two structural remarks explain the profile. First, rotations are \emph{not} the distance-hard nonexpansive instances: the averaged operator $\tfrac12(I+R_\varphi)$ has spectral radius $\cos(\varphi/2)<1$, so constant-parameter blocks converge geometrically at rate $\varphi^{2}/8$ --- it is anchoring toward $x_0$, not the rotation, that slows the classical schedule. Second, the classical blocks do \emph{not} restore residual-optimality on $\mathcal{N}$: within a block the weights $1/(n+2)$ are $\asymp 2^{-j}$ and nearly constant, so a block damps the residual by at most a constant factor on worst-case nonexpansive maps. The schedule is genuinely a two-regime (contraction--rotation) object, and Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel} remains the only proved route to the residual-optimal $O(D/\varepsilon)$ on $\mathcal{N}$. \emph{Target dependence.} The small-mass parameter $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)$ and all stopping horizons in this paper depend on the target $\varepsilon$ and the bound $D$; this is standard --- the classical schedule itself is only certified up to the horizon $N=\lceil4D/\varepsilon\rceil$ --- and no schedule here ever uses the modulus. Consequently \emph{the factor-$2$ overhead of Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel} is an artifact of the two-constant-density-track design, not a barrier}: the deterministic necessity question for single-stream schemes is resolved in the negative --- one fixed oscillatory stream, no parallelism, no adaptivity, no overhead, optimal on contractions and rotation-polynomial (its residual behavior on $\mathcal{N}$ is the open part, (c) above). (The \emph{stochastic} single-stream analogue of Theorem~\ref{thm:stoch-parallel} remains open; Remark~\ref{rem:stoch-parallel-open}.)
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
\label{rem:oscillatory}
Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} disposes of the case $\liminf c_n=0<\limsup c_n$: the conjecture that the dichotomy still holds is \emph{false}. The remaining regime is exactly $M(n)\asymp\ln n$ with every superlevel set $\{k:c_k\ge c'\}$ lacunary (gaps tending to infinity), and there the explicit schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} --- classical anchor mass on dyadic half-blocks, mass $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)$ on the complementary stretches --- is simultaneously contraction-optimal and rotation-polynomial with the lacunarity exponent $1/\alpha_0$, $\alpha_0=\log_2(3/2)$ (upper bound proved in Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}(ii); matching lower bound given for Lebesgue-a.e.\ angle in Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}, with the pointwise statement open beyond Diophantine angles). The mechanism, made precise in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}(ii): each dyadic scale multiplies the anchor residue $r_n$ by $\approx(2/3)(3/4)^{c'}=2^{-\alpha}$, and the chord bound on the kernel shows the de-rotated error follows the envelope $(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}$ up to the stretch contributions, which are mass-small once $n\gtrsim\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha}$. On the contraction side the small-mass stretches run the small-anchor dynamics of Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c} (exploiting contractivity at gap $\delta$ once the stretch length exceeds $\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta$). The dense scales (the blocks) re-inject anchor mass at scale $D/(k+2)$, but only at lacunary positions $k\asymp2^j$; the lacunarity index $\alpha$ is exactly the reciprocal of the escape exponent, and the error dips below $\varepsilon$ before the next re-injection can matter. The boundary is \emph{lacunarity of the dense anchor scales}: bounded gaps force the dichotomy (Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-bounded-gap}), lacunary gaps break it (Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}).
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:isolation}
Let $w_n := \langle y_n,u_n \rangle\in\mathbb{C}$ in the de-rotated frame. Since $u_{n+1}=e^{-i\varphi}u_n$,
$$w_{n+1}\;=\;e^{i\varphi}\bigl((1-\theta_n)w_n+\theta_n\bigr),\qquad\text{hence}\qquad |w_{n+1}|\;\ge\;\theta_n-(1-\theta_n)|w_n|\;\ge\;\frac{\theta_n}{2}\quad\text{whenever }|w_n|\le\frac{\theta_n}{2}.$$
As $\norm{x_n}=\norm{y_n}\ge|w_n|$, no two consecutive iterates satisfy $\norm{x_n}<\theta_n/2$: deep crossings of the orbit below the moving threshold $\theta_n/2\asymp 1/n$ are isolated in $n$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:orbit-smallball}
Fix $\alpha\in[\alpha_0,3/5]$ and the envelope constants $c_r,C_r$ of $r_n$; recall $\kappa_k=\theta_k/r_{k+1}=1/r_{k+1}-1/r_k$ and $Z_n:=\widehat K_n+w_n'$, so that $\norm{x_n}=r_n2^{j_0\alpha}|Z_n|$. There is a universal constant $C_N$ (depending only on $c_r,C_r,\alpha$) such that for every $j_0\ge6$, every $m$ with $\Delta:=m-j_0\in\{-4,\dots,0\}$, and every $n\in[2^m,2^{m+1})$, writing $\varphi=2^{-j_0}\psi$, $\rho:=\sin(\varphi/2)$ ($\rho\asymp2^{-j_0}$), and $\omega:=2^{\Delta-1}\psi$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:trig-form}
\rho Z_n(\psi)\;=\;P_n(\omega)+E_n(\omega),\qquad |E_n|\;\le\;\rho\,C_N\quad\text{on }(\pi,2\pi],
\end{equation}
where $P_n$ is an explicit trigonometric polynomial: with $W_i:=\bigl((2^i+1)r_{2^i}\bigr)^{-1}$ and $c_i:=2^{-j_0\alpha}W_i$,
\begin{eqnarray*}
P_n(\omega)\;&=&\;\frac{c_m}{\rho}\,e^{i\Lambda_m(\omega)}\sin(\tau\omega/2)\;+\;\frac{c_{m-1}}{\rho}\,e^{i\Lambda_{m-1}(\omega)}\sin(\omega/4)\;\\
&&+\;\sum_{i\le m-2}\Bigl[\frac{c_i}{\rho}\,e^{i\Lambda_i(\omega)}\sin(2^{i-m-1}\omega)+\mathrm{stretch}_i(\omega)\Bigr],
\end{eqnarray*}
$\tau:=(n-2^m)/2^{m-1}\in[0,1]$, the phases $\Lambda_i$ are affine in $\omega$ with leading coefficient $2^{i-m+1}$ (hence slow for $i\le m-2$), $\mathrm{stretch}_i$ is the corresponding slowly amplitude-modulated stretch chord with $|\mathrm{stretch}_i|\le 2^{-j_0\alpha}\min\{2^{i\alpha},\,2^{i\alpha-1}/\rho\}\cdot O(1)$, and
$$c_i\;\asymp\;\frac{2^{-j_0+(i-j_0)(\alpha-1)}}{c_r},\qquad\text{in particular }c_{m-1}\asymp2^{\alpha-1}c_m,\quad c_m\asymp\frac{2^{-j_0+\Delta(\alpha-1)}}{c_r}.$$
Three features of \eqref{eq:trig-form} block the single-term Rouch\'e route to the orbit small-ball: the leading coefficients $c_m$ and $c_{m-1}$ are comparable, so the ``perturbation'' is the same order as the ``main term''; the top frequencies $\tau/2$ and $1/4$ are not separated, so resonant cancellation cannot be excluded; and on deeper blocks ($m>j_0$) the sub-handoff tail is of the same order as the main term and its derivative exceeds the main slope by a factor $2^{m-j_0}$. The orbit small-ball itself is stated as Conjecture~\ref{conj:orbit-smallball} below.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
On a classical block $B_i=\{2^i,\dots,3\cdot2^{i-1}-1\}$, $\theta_k=1/(k+2)$ gives $r_{k+1}=r_{2^i}(2^i+1)/(k+2)$, hence $\kappa_k\equiv W_i$, and the geometric sum is exact:
$$\sum_{k\in B_i}e^{ik\varphi}\;=\;e^{i(2^i+2^{i-2}-\frac12)\varphi}\,\frac{\sin(2^{i-2}\varphi)}{\rho}.$$
Since $2^{i-2}\varphi=2^{i-m-1}\omega$ and $(2^i+2^{i-2}-\frac12)\varphi=2^{i-m+1}\omega+(2^{i-j_0-2}-\varepsilon_0)\psi$ with $\varepsilon_0:=2^{-j_0-1}$, this is exactly the block-chord displayed above. On a stretch $S_i$, $\kappa_k=c'(k+2)^{c'-1}/\bigl((3\cdot2^{i-1}+1)^{c'}r_{3\cdot2^{i-1}}\bigr)$ varies by at most a factor $2$, and Abel summation against the geometric series gives the slowly amplitude-modulated chord satisfying the stated bound. Taking $E_n:=\rho(N_n+w_n')$, where $N_n$ is the transform of all pieces below the top three scales,
$$|N_n|\;\le\;2^{-j_0\alpha}\!\!\sum_{k<3\cdot2^{m-2}}\!\!\kappa_k\;=\;2^{-j_0\alpha}\Bigl(\frac{1}{r_{3\cdot2^{m-2}}}-1\Bigr)\;\le\;\frac{2^{\alpha(\Delta-2)}}{c_r}\;\le\;\frac{2^{-2\alpha}}{c_r},$$
using $1/r_j\le c_r^{-1}j^\alpha$ and $\Delta\le0$; together with $|w_n'|=2^{-j_0\alpha}$ this gives $|E_n|\le\rho C_N$. Finally $c_r2^{-i\alpha}\le r_{2^i}\le C_r2^{-i\alpha}$ and $2^i<2^i+1\le2^{i+1}$ give $2^{i(\alpha-1)}/(2C_r)\le W_i\le2^{i(\alpha-1)}/c_r$, hence the comparison for $c_i$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- conjecture-1: body_md -->
\label{conj:orbit-smallball}
In the setting of Lemma~\ref{lem:orbit-smallball}, there exist $\bar\mu>0$ and $C<\infty$ (depending only on $c_r,C_r,\alpha$) such that for every $j_0\ge j_*$, every $n\ge2^{j_0}$, and every $0<\mu\le\bar\mu$,
$$\bigl|\{\psi\in(\pi,2\pi]:\norm{x_n(2^{-j_0}\psi)}<\mu\,r_n\,2^{j_0\alpha}\}\bigr|\;\le\;C\mu .$$
<!-- end conjecture-1 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:metric-lacunarity}
For the schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}, with $\alpha(\varepsilon)=\alpha_0+O(\varepsilon)$ and $\varphi_* := \pi2^{-j_*+1}$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{A.e.\ exact rate at each fixed accuracy; full-density limit.} For every fixed $\varepsilon>0$ there is, for Lebesgue-a.e.\ $\varphi\in(0,\varphi_0]$, a constant $\mu(\varphi,\varepsilon)>0$ such that
$$N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\varphi^{-1}\,\mu(\varphi,\varepsilon)\,\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha(\varepsilon)}.$$
The bad set $B=\{(\varphi,\varepsilon):\text{the display fails}\}$ is Borel (see the proof), so Tonelli gives: for Lebesgue-a.e.\ $\varphi$, the accuracies at which the display holds have full Lebesgue density near $0$. Since the matching upper bound $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)=O\bigl(\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha(\varepsilon)}\bigr)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}(ii) holds for every $\varphi$ and every $\varepsilon$, it follows that for Lebesgue-a.e.\ $\varphi$,
$$\limsup_{\varepsilon\to0}\frac{\ln N_\varphi(\varepsilon)}{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}\;\le\;\frac{1}{\alpha_0},
\qquad
\frac{\ln N_\varphi(\varepsilon)}{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}\;\ge\;\frac{1}{\alpha_0}-o(1)
\ \text{on a full-density set of accuracies},$$
i.e.\ $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)=\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0+o(1)}$ along a full-density set of accuracies. The unrestricted limit over all sequences $\varepsilon\to0$ is equivalent to the fixed-constant envelope (locking) statement {\rm(iv)} of Remark~\ref{rem:lacunarity-status} and remains open.
\item \emph{Uniform bound, non-explicit rate.} For every $\beta<1/\alpha_0$ and every $\varphi_1 \in [\varphi_*,\varphi_0]$ there exists $c(\beta)>0$ such that the set
$$\mathrm{Bad}(\varepsilon) \;:=\; \bigl\{\varphi \in (\varphi_1,\varphi_0] :\; N_\varphi(\varepsilon) \;<\; c(\beta)\,\varphi^{-1}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}\bigr\}$$
has Lebesgue measure $o(1)$ as $\varepsilon\to0$. Moreover, for a.e.\ $\varphi\in(0,\varphi_0]$ and every $\beta<1/\alpha_0$, $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\ge c(\beta,\varphi)\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-\beta}$ for all $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon(\varphi,\beta)$ (this consequence follows already from clause (i), which gives it with the exact exponent $1/\alpha_0$; the restriction $\varphi_1\ge\varphi_*$ is immaterial for the a.e.\ statement, since clause (i) covers the shallowest shells). An explicit decay rate $|\mathrm{Bad}(\varepsilon)|\le C\varepsilon^\kappa$ would follow from the orbit small-ball (Conjecture~\ref{conj:orbit-smallball}) and is open, see Remark~\ref{rem:lacunarity-status}.
\item \emph{Coherence floor.} For every $\varphi\le\pi/3$, $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\ge\pi/(3\varphi)$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
\emph{Proof of (i) and (iii).} Clause (iii) is Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor}. For (i), fix a shell $E_{j_0}$. The coefficients $\kappa_k$ are nonnegative, tend to $0$, and have bounded variation: the variation across each stretch $S_i$ is $O(2^{i(\alpha-1)})$ and $\alpha<1$, so $\sum_k|\kappa_{k+1}-\kappa_k|<\infty$. By Dirichlet's test $\sum_k\kappa_ke^{ik\varphi}$ converges uniformly on compact subsets of $(0,2\pi)$ to a real-analytic limit $K_\infty(\varphi)$. The limit is not identically zero: if it vanished on an interval, the identity theorem would force it to vanish on all of $(0,2\pi)$, and Cantor's uniqueness theorem for trigonometric series (Appendix~\ref{app:certificate}) would give $\kappa_k=0$ for all $k$, contradicting $\sum_k\kappa_k=\lim_n(r_n^{-1}-1)=\infty$. The set
$$\mathcal Z_{j_0}:=\{\varphi\in E_{j_0}: K_\infty(\varphi)=0\}\cup\{\varphi\in E_{j_0}: |K_\infty(\varphi)|=1\}\cup\bigcup_{n\ge1}\{\varphi:\norm{x_n(\varphi)}=0\}\cup\mathcal R_{j_0}$$
is therefore null, where $\mathcal R_{j_0}$ is the countable set of rational multiples $\varphi=2\pi p/q$ for which $K_\infty(\varphi)=-e^{-2\pi ipj/q}$ for some $j\in\{0,\dots,q-1\}$ (each such equation is analytic and is not an identity on $E_{j_0}$: $K_\infty\equiv-e^{-i(n-1)\varphi}$ would force $\kappa_k=0$ for $k\neq n-1$ by Cantor's theorem, contradicting $\kappa_k\ge0$ and $\sum\kappa_k=\infty$; so each equation has only isolated solutions). For $\varphi\in E_{j_0}\setminus\mathcal Z_{j_0}$: since $|K_n(\varphi)-K_\infty(\varphi)|\to0$ and, for $\varphi/2\pi$ irrational, $e^{-i(n-1)\varphi}$ is dense on the unit circle,
$$\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,\bigl|K_n(\varphi)+e^{-i(n-1)\varphi}\bigr|\;=\;\min_{\theta}\,\bigl|K_\infty(\varphi)+e^{i\theta}\bigr|\;=\;\bigl||K_\infty(\varphi)|-1\bigr|\;=:\;d(\varphi)\;>\;0,$$
and for rational angles off $\mathcal R_{j_0}$ the same holds with the minimum over the finite orbit $\{e^{-i(n-1)\varphi}\}_n$. Hence, with $r_n\ge c_rn^{-\alpha}$,
$$\liminf_{n\to\infty}\,\norm{x_n}\,(n\varphi)^{\alpha}\;\ge\;\varphi^{\alpha}\,c_r\,d(\varphi)\;=:\;\mu_1(\varphi)\;>\;0,$$
so $\norm{x_n}>\varepsilon$ for all $n\le\varphi^{-1}\bigl(\mu_1(\varphi)/\varepsilon\bigr)^{1/\alpha}$ once $\varepsilon$ is small enough that the right side exceeds the finitely many transient stages. This proves the display of clause (i) at the fixed accuracy $\varepsilon$, with $\mu(\varphi,\varepsilon):=\mu_1(\varphi)^{1/\alpha(\varepsilon)}$, for every $\varphi\in E_{j_0}\setminus\mathcal Z_{j_0}$; since $\bigcup_{j_0}\mathcal Z_{j_0}$ is null, the display holds for Lebesgue-a.e.\ $\varphi\in(0,\varphi_0]$ at this fixed $\varepsilon$. For the density statement, extend $K_\infty$ by $0$ at $c'=0$: the Dirichlet convergence of $\sum_k\kappa_k(c')e^{ik\varphi}$ is uniform on compact subsets of $(0,2\pi)\times[0,c_0]$ (because $\sup_{k\ge K}\kappa_k(c')\le C\max\{c',\,2^{-K(1-\alpha(c_0))}\}$ and $\sum_k|\kappa_{k+1}(c')-\kappa_k(c')|$ is bounded uniformly in $c'$), and each $x_n(\varphi,c')$ is jointly analytic in $(\varphi,c')$; hence the bad set $B$ is Borel, and Tonelli gives $\int|B_\varphi|\,d\varphi=|B|=\int|\mathcal Z(\varepsilon)|\,d\varepsilon=0$.

\emph{Proof of (ii).} Fix $\beta<1/\alpha_0$ and a shell $E_{j_0}$; it suffices to bound $\mathrm{Bad}(\varepsilon)\cap E_{j_0}$. Let $K_\infty$ be the orbit limit of clause (i) (Appendix~\ref{app:certificate}: $K_n\to K_\infty$ uniformly on compact subsets of $(0,2\pi)$, $K_\infty$ real-analytic, $K_\infty\not\equiv0$, and $|K_\infty|\not\equiv1$ --- the last because $|K_\infty|^2\equiv1$ would force the convolution sums $\sum_k\kappa_k\kappa_{k+d}$ to vanish for every $d\ge1$, impossible for $\kappa_k\ge0$ with $\sum_k\kappa_k=\infty$). Hence $\{|K_\infty|=1\}$ is null, and for every $\delta>0$ the set $\{\,||K_\infty|-1|<\delta\,\}$ has measure $m_0(\delta)\to0$ as $\delta\downarrow0$. By Egorov's theorem choose a compact $C\subset(\pi,2\pi]$ with $|(\pi,2\pi]\setminus C|\le\eta$ ($\eta>0$ arbitrary) on which $K_n\to K_\infty$ uniformly, and then $n_0$ such that $|K_n-K_\infty|\le\delta/4$ on $C$ for all $n\ge n_0$. For $\psi\in C':=C\cap\{\,||K_\infty|-1|\ge\delta\,\}$ and all $n\ge n_0$,
$$\norm{x_n}\;=\;r_n\bigl|K_n(\varphi)+e^{-i(n-1)\varphi}\bigr|\;\ge\;r_n\Bigl(\bigl||K_\infty(\varphi)|-1\bigr|-\tfrac{\delta}{4}\Bigr)\;\ge\;\tfrac{3\delta}{4}\,c_r n^{-\alpha},$$
by the triangle inequality (no equidistribution is needed at this step). Consequently $\norm{x_n}>\varepsilon$ for all $n\le(3\delta c_r/(4\varepsilon))^{1/\alpha}$, i.e.
$$N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\Bigl(\frac{3\delta c_r}{4\varepsilon}\Bigr)^{1/\alpha}\qquad(\psi\in C'),$$
which is incompatible with $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)<c(\beta)\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-\beta}$ as soon as $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_1(\beta,\delta)$, since $\beta<1/\alpha_0$ and $1/\alpha(\varepsilon)\to1/\alpha_0$ as $\varepsilon\to0$. The transient indices $n<n_0$ contribute $\bigcup_{n<n_0}\{\norm{x_n}\le\varepsilon\}$, a set of measure $o(1)$ as $\varepsilon\to0$ because each $\norm{x_n(\cdot)}$ is real-analytic and not identically zero. Hence $\limsup_{\varepsilon\to0}|\mathrm{Bad}(\varepsilon)\cap E_{j_0}|\le\eta+m_0(\delta)$, and $\eta,\delta$ are arbitrary.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- conjecture-2: body_md -->
\label{conj:pointwise-lacunarity}
If the doubling orbit of $\varphi/2\pi$ satisfies the quantitative equidistribution bound $D_M(\varphi) \le C(\varphi)\,M^{-1/2}\log M$ (discrepancy of $(2^j\varphi \bmod 2\pi)_{j\le M}$), then for every $\beta < 1/\alpha_0$,
$$N_\varphi(\varepsilon) \;\ge\; c(\beta,\varphi)\,\varphi^{-1}\,\varepsilon^{-\beta}\qquad\text{for all } \varepsilon \in (0,\varepsilon_0(\beta)).$$
<!-- end conjecture-2 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
\label{rem:greedy-numerics}
The greedy adversarial construction --- grid minimization of $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)/(\varphi^{-1}\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha_0})$ over a dyadic shell --- was stress-tested at $\varepsilon \in \{0.1, 0.05, 0.02\}$ on the shell $2^{-7}[\pi,2\pi)$ (grid $480$), with the simulation verified exactly against Table~\ref{tab:oscillatory}. Deep dips are realized at computable scales, but only in the transition octave and on a small Cantor set of angles: adversarial angles $\psi^\star \approx 3.97, 4.07, 4.09$ admit dips to ratio $6\cdot10^{-4}$--$7\cdot10^{-2}$ of the envelope at the first handoff $n\varphi \approx 11.6$ (bad-set measure $\sim2\cdot10^{-3}$ per shell); for each fixed adversarial angle the dip does not recur at deeper octaves (min-ratio $0.11$--$0.9$ across $n\varphi\in[2^4,2^{17}]$), but each scale has its own adversarial angles and refining the Cantor descent deepens the transition dips --- forcing the universal constant in any pointwise claim toward $0$. We therefore assess the universal pointwise statement as \emph{likely false}, and Conjecture~\ref{conj:pointwise-lacunarity} (Diophantine angles) together with Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity} as the correct replacement. These numerics admit two readings. The \emph{threshold} events $\{\norm{x_n}\le\varepsilon\}$ are rare at a rate decaying with $\varepsilon$ (measured frequency $\sim 10^{-2}$ at $\varepsilon = 0.02$, decreasing with shell depth), consistent with the small-ball intuition of Conjecture~\ref{conj:orbit-smallball} and with the uniform $o(1)$ bad-set bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(ii). The \emph{fixed-constant envelope} events $\{\norm{x_n} < c_0(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}\}$ have measure $\sim c_0$ each, and their union over $n$, without the anti-dependence (``locking'') observed in simulation, would give only bad-set measure $\sim c_0\log(1/\varepsilon)$; proving the fixed-envelope statement a.e.\ would require proving that locking, which we leave open (see Remark~\ref{rem:lacunarity-status}). Second, the numerics forced the exponent correction: because $c'=\varepsilon/(96D)$, the achievable exponent is $1/\alpha(\varepsilon) = 1/\alpha_0 - O(\varepsilon)$, so the a.e.\ exponent $1/\alpha_0$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(i) is the best possible, while the uniform bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(ii) gives bad sets of measure $o(1)$ for every $\beta<1/\alpha_0$ without an explicit rate; explicit rates, in the direction of the full exponent $1/\alpha_0$, require the orbit small-ball (Conjecture~\ref{conj:orbit-smallball}) and are open (Remark~\ref{rem:lacunarity-status}).
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
\label{rem:lacunarity-status}
We distinguish four statements. \emph{(i) The a.e.\ hitting-time rate is proved with the exact exponent at each fixed accuracy (Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(i)):} for a.e.\ $\varphi$ and every fixed $\varepsilon$, $N_\varphi(\varepsilon)\ge\varphi^{-1}\mu(\varphi,\varepsilon)\varepsilon^{-1/\alpha(\varepsilon)}$, matching Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}(ii) up to the constant, and by Tonelli the bound holds on a full-density set of accuracies for a.e.\ $\varphi$; the constant-density case remains fully explicit (Lemma~\ref{lem:envelope} and Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(ii)), and the $\varphi$-exponent is proved for every schedule and every angle (Lemma~\ref{lem:rot-floor}). \emph{(ii) The uniform bound holds with a non-explicit rate for every $\beta<1/\alpha_0$} (Theorem~\ref{thm:metric-lacunarity}(ii), proved by Egorov uniformity and the analyticity of $K_\infty$); the explicit-rate version would follow from the orbit small-ball (Conjecture~\ref{conj:orbit-smallball}). Three features of Lemma~\ref{lem:orbit-smallball} block the single-term Rouch\'e analysis from delivering that small-ball: the leading chord coefficients are comparable, the top chord frequencies are not separated, and on deeper blocks the sub-handoff tail is of the same order as the main term; a genuine grouped/lacunary small-ball for the full orbit transform (of Sidon type, cf.\ \cite{Kahane1985,Zygmund2002}) is the missing ingredient. Reaching $1/\alpha_0$ uniformly with explicit constants is open. \emph{(iii) The pointwise law survives only for Diophantine angles} (Conjecture~\ref{conj:pointwise-lacunarity}) and is likely false universally (Remark~\ref{rem:greedy-numerics}). \emph{(iv) The fixed-constant uniform envelope} $\norm{x_n}\ge c_0(n\varphi)^{-\alpha}$ with $c_0$ independent of $\varepsilon$ is the statement connected with locking: its natural proof (the dyadic-chaining argument across scales $z_{j+1}=\rho_jz_j+f_j$ with forcing $|f_j|\le\min\{1/3, 2/(n_j\varphi)\}$) fails at the handoff scale $n\varphi\asymp1$, where worst-case cancellation is vacuous, and the union bound without locking gives only measure $\sim c_0\log(1/\varepsilon)$. We do not know whether the fixed-envelope statement holds for a.e.\ $\varphi$; it is not needed anywhere in this paper. The mechanism behind the whole lacunarity analysis is the schedule's rotation Fourier spectrum: at threshold $\varepsilon$, rare cancellations of the orbit sum at radius $\mu_n(\varepsilon) \asymp \varepsilon/(r_n2^{j_0\alpha})$ control the hitting time (Lemma~\ref{lem:orbit-smallball}), and as $c\to0$ the prefactor $\Gamma(c+1)\to1$ while the exponent $-c\to0$ quantifies the damping.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:parallel}
Fix $\varepsilon<D/4$ and $D=\norm{x_0-x^*}$. Run in parallel, from the same anchor $x_0$, two anchored iterations \eqref{eq:anchored}:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
\item \emph{Track A} (contraction track): the schedule $\theta_n=c/(n+2)$ with $c=\varepsilon/(96D)$, as in Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c};
\item \emph{Track B} (nonexpansive track): the classical Halpern schedule $\theta_n=1/(n+2)$.
\end{itemize}
Define the hitting time of the scheme as the first $n$ at which \emph{either} track satisfies the class-appropriate accuracy test ($\norm{x_n-x^*}\le\varepsilon$ on $\mathcal{C}$, residual $\norm{x_n-Tx_n}\le\varepsilon$ on $\mathcal{N}$). Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item on every $\gamma$-contraction, the hitting time is at most $1+4\ln(2D/(\varepsilon\gamma))/\ln(1/\gamma)=O\bigl(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/(1-\gamma)\bigr)$ --- matching the $\gamma$-aware deterministic optimum for span-class algorithms \cite{ParkRyu2022};
\item on every nonexpansive map, the hitting time is at most $1+2D/\varepsilon$ --- matching the optimal Halpern rate up to the absolute constant in \eqref{eq:lieder} \cite{Lieder2021,Contreras2023}.
\end{enumerate}
The scheme evaluates each track once per step (a factor $2$ in evaluations over a single track) and is otherwise non-adaptive: it is a parallel composition of two fixed, $\gamma$-oblivious schedules.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Track A on $\mathcal{C}_\gamma$ is Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c} verbatim (the schedule contains no $\gamma$). Track B on $\mathcal{N}$ is the sharp residual bound $\norm{x_n-Tx_n}\le2D/(n+1)$ of \cite{Lieder2021,Contreras2023}. The scheme stops at the minimum of the two hitting times, so on each class it inherits the better bound.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
\label{rem:complete-dichotomy}
Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel} is the sharp converse to Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}: \emph{no fixed schedule} is simultaneously contraction-optimal and rotation-polynomial (proved for every density $c_n\in(0,1]$), while \emph{some two-track scheme} is simultaneously contraction-optimal and nonexpansive-optimal, at a factor $2$ in evaluations --- and, among oscillatory densities, even a \emph{single fixed schedule} achieves both regimes (Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}), so the necessity of adaptivity is exactly the necessity of non-lacunary anchor concentration. The factor $2$ is the price of not knowing the class in advance --- and Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} shows it is \emph{not} necessary: a single fixed oscillatory stream achieves both regimes, indeed all three (Remark~\ref{rem:osc-profile}), with no overhead. The deterministic overhead question is therefore resolved in the negative; the parallel scheme remains the simplest sufficiency witness.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:single-instance}
Let $\theta_n=c_n/(n+2)$ with $c_n\in(0,1]$, and measure accuracy by distance to the fixed point. In case {\rm(i)} of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general} ($\liminf c_n\ge c_0>0$), the \emph{single} instance
$$T_\star\;=\;\rho\,\mathrm{id}\,\oplus\,R_\varphi\ \ \text{on }\mathbb{R}^2,\qquad \rho\in[\tfrac12,1),\ \ \varphi\in(0,\pi],\qquad x_0=\tfrac{(D,D)}{\sqrt2},$$
forces
\begin{equation}\label{eq:single-instance}
N_{T_\star}(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\Bigl(\frac{c_0\,\rho D}{32e^{3}\,\varepsilon(1-\rho)}\Bigr)^{1/3},
\end{equation}
polynomially suboptimal on this one instance relative to the $\gamma$-aware optimum $\Theta(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/(1-\rho))$ by the factor of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}(i). In case {\rm(ii)(b)}, the single rotation $T_\star=R_{\varphi_B(\varepsilon)}$ forces $N_{T_\star}(\varepsilon)>\varepsilon^{-B}$ for every $B\ge1$; in case {\rm(ii)(a)}, some rotation is never solved. Hence \emph{every fixed schedule is slow on a single nonexpansive instance}: the difficulty is a property of the instance, not of the schedule's interaction with a family.
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Coordinate $1$ of $T_\star$ evolves exactly as the scalar anchored iteration on $T=\rho\,\mathrm{id}$ with anchor $D/\sqrt2$ and the same coefficients: $x_{1,n+1}=\theta_n x_{1,0}+(1-\theta_n)\rho x_{1,n}$. Hence Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor-floor} and the computation of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}(i) (with $D$ replaced by $D/\sqrt2$ and $r_N\ge(2N)^{-2}$) give $|x_{1,N}|\ge c_0\rho(D/\sqrt2)/(16e^{3}N^{3}(1-\rho))$ for $N$ large, and since $\mathrm{Fix}(T_\star)=\{0\}$, $\norm{x_N-x^*}\ge|x_{1,N}|$; \eqref{eq:single-instance} follows (using $16\sqrt2\le32$). Cases {\rm(ii)} are Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}(ii) verbatim.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\label{rem:overhead-obstructions}
The factor-$2$ question (Remark~\ref{rem:complete-dichotomy}) survives the following three rigorous obstructions, which together delimit what any proof must avoid. \emph{(a) Response-matching pairs cannot affect hitting times.} Two instances agreeing on every queried point produce identical trajectories, and the resistant-oracle construction (Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility}, valid for $\sigma=0$) provides such pairs with $1-\mathrm{Lip}(T)$ differing by any prescribed factor --- but the construction keeps $x^*=0$ for both instances (the nominal is intercept-free and the bumps sit off the probed set), so the common trajectory converges to the \emph{same} fixed point and the hitting time is identical: the pair is useless for a hitting-time lower bound. \emph{(b) Angles distinguish the scalar families.} Against the exact oracle, $\arccos\langle x_n,Tx_n \rangle/(\norm{x_n}\norm{Tx_n})$ is $0$ on $T=\rho\,\mathrm{id}$ and $\varphi$ on $R_\varphi$: no lower bound can treat these families as indistinguishable, which is why the detection barrier (Remark~\ref{rem:detection}) must hide slopes in \emph{unprobed directions} rather than rely on response matching --- and obstruction (a) shows that hiding cannot move $x^*$. \emph{(c) The control is one-dimensional.} A scheme confined to \eqref{eq:anchored} acts through the single scalar $\theta_n$; on the direct-sum instance of Corollary~\ref{cor:single-instance}, one schedule must serve both coordinates, and the floors apply coordinatewise to the \emph{realized} schedule. For fixed schedules this yields Corollary~\ref{cor:single-instance}; for adaptive schemes the realized schedule depends on $(\rho,\varphi)$ through the full response history, and the quantile argument of Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility} does not control it. The sharpened question is therefore: on the direct-sum instances $T=\rho\,\mathrm{id}\oplus R_\varphi$, prove that every adaptive anchored single-stream scheme with worst-case contraction time $t(\delta)=O(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta)$ has, for some $(\rho,\varphi)$, hitting time super-polynomially larger than $\min\{t(\delta),D/\varepsilon\}$ --- or exhibit a single-stream scheme matching Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel}.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
\label{rem:stopping}
Throughout Section~\ref{sec:det}, hitting times are the minimax-analytic objects being lower- and upper-bounded, and Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel} answers Q1 in exactly this sense. If instead the scheme must \emph{certify} from oracle data that its output is $\varepsilon$-accurate, the picture changes qualitatively: residual accuracy on $\mathcal{N}$ is self-certifying (the criterion is itself a residual), but distance accuracy on $\mathcal{C}_\gamma$ requires one-sided modulus information (Remark~\ref{rem:barrier}), and with a stochastic oracle no positive sound certificate exists without a certified ceiling, in any dimension (Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility}). The parallel scheme is therefore a sharp answer to Q1 as a deterministic complexity question; its stochastic counterpart must be phrased through the trichotomy of Section~\ref{sec:contraction} --- which is precisely what that trichotomy is for.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:doubling-window}
Define iterates $y_0=x_0$ and, for $k=0,1,2,\dots$, window $k$ of length $L_k=2^{k+3}$: starting from $y_k$, run
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dw-window}
x^{(k)}_{j+1}\;=\;\tfrac{1}{j+2}\,y_k\;+\;\tfrac{j+1}{j+2}\,T x^{(k)}_j,\qquad j=0,\dots,L_k-1,\qquad x^{(k)}_0=y_k,
\end{equation}
and set $y_{k+1}=x^{(k)}_{L_k}$. Write $d_k=\norm{y_k-x^*}$ for any fixed point $x^*$ (on $\mathcal{C}_\gamma$ the unique one). Then, for $T\in\mathcal{N}\cup\bigcup_\gamma\mathcal{C}_\gamma$, $\norm{x_0-x^*}\le D$, $\varepsilon<D$:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Nonexpansive regime (residual).} $\norm{y_{k+1}-Ty_{k+1}}\le D/2^{k+2}$ for every $k$: in particular the scheme certifies residual $\le\varepsilon$ within $16D/\varepsilon$ iterations, the optimal $O(D/\varepsilon)$ order.
\item \emph{Contraction regime (distance).} For every $\gamma$-contraction, writing $\delta=1-\gamma$,
$$d_{k+1}\;\le\;\frac{d_k}{\delta(L_k+1)}\quad\text{for all }k,\qquad\text{hence}\qquad \norm{y_k-x^*}\;\le\;\varepsilon\ \text{ by window } m+\bigl\lceil\log_2(D/\varepsilon)\bigr\rceil,$$
where $m=\min\{k:\delta L_k\ge2\}$; the total iteration count to reach distance $\le\varepsilon$ is $O\bigl(D/(\varepsilon\delta)\bigr)$. This is the \emph{same order} as the classical schedule's contraction time (Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(i) gives the matching lower bound $N_\rho(\varepsilon)\ge D(1-2\varepsilon)/(2(1-\rho)\varepsilon)$ for the classical schedule): window-restart adaptivity on a single trajectory improves the constant (by a factor $\approx3$ in our experiments) but \emph{not the order}, and in particular does not approach the optimal $O(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta)$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c}. The separation of Theorem~\ref{thm:separation} is therefore robust against this natural adaptive class.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
