Within window $k$ the iteration \eqref{eq:dw-window} is the classical Halpern iteration anchored at $y_k$; since $\norm{x^{(k)}_{j+1}-x^*}\le\max\{d_k,\norm{x^{(k)}_j-x^*}\}$ (both $y_k$ and $Tx^{(k)}_j$ are within $d_k$ of $x^*$), induction gives $d_{k+1}\le d_k\le D$, and the sharp residual bound of \cite{Lieder2021,Contreras2023} gives (i). For (ii), the recursion $e_{j+1}\le\frac{1}{j+2}d_k+\frac{j+1}{j+2}\gamma e_j$, $e_0=d_k$, unfolds (by the same induction as Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(i), whose steps are inequalities valid for every contraction) to $d_{k+1}\le d_k\,(1-\gamma^{L_k+1})/(\delta(L_k+1))\le d_k/(\delta(L_k+1))$. Hence for $k\ge m=\min\{k:\delta L_k\ge2\}$ we have $d_k\le D\,2^{-(k-m)}$, so $d_k\le\varepsilon$ by window $m+\lceil\log_2(D/\varepsilon)\rceil$, having used $\sum_{i\le k}L_i<2^{k+4}\le2^{m+4}\cdot 2(D/\varepsilon)=O(D/(\varepsilon\delta))$ iterations (using $2^{m}\le4/\delta$, as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-bounded-gap}).
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
\label{rem:dw-buys}
On contractions the scheme's $O(D/(\varepsilon\delta))$ distance time matches the classical schedule's order (Theorem~\ref{thm:separation}(i)) with a better constant: in Experiment~4 the measured hitting times on $T(x)=0.95x$ at $\varepsilon=0.05$ are $59$ (small-anchor), $142$ (doubling-window; first iterate inside a window --- window ends: $248$) and $399$ (classical). The mechanism is visible in the display of Theorem~\ref{thm:doubling-window}(ii): within window $k$ the distance decays by the factor $1/(\delta L_k)$, and the windows double, so the error decays like $1/n$ overall --- exactly the classical order. No growing-window scheme on a single trajectory is known to do better while retaining the $O(D/\varepsilon)$ nonexpansive guarantee; the heuristic barrier is that exploiting contractivity at gap $\delta$ requires windows of length $\Omega(1/\delta)$, and the union class contains contractions of every $\delta$ --- but we do not have a proof that growing windows cannot succeed. Together with the detection barrier (Remark~\ref{rem:detection}) and the oscillatory escape (Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape}), this locates exactly where single-trajectory optimality can live: not in statistic-based detection (barred), not in growing-window restarts (only constant-factor), but in \emph{oscillation of the anchor density} --- which achieves it, without any adaptation at all (Remark~\ref{rem:osc-profile}).
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
\label{rem:detection}
The parallel scheme of Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel} sidesteps detection entirely, at factor-$2$ cost. A single-trajectory scheme must instead decide, from its own trajectory, when to exploit contractivity; two barriers prevent the naive detector. \emph{(a) Hidden directions (deterministic).} Even with exact oracle responses, every probe $x\mapsto Tx$ reveals only directional slopes $\norm{Tx-Ty}/\norm{x-y}\le\gamma$: an adversary can hide the attaining slope in a direction the trajectory never probes. Concretely, the construction of Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility} works verbatim without noise ($\sigma=0$): the nominal $T_0(x)=\gamma_0x$ and the modified $T$ agree on the entire probed set, so no algorithm whose decisions depend on oracle responses can distinguish them, yet $1-\mathrm{Lip}(T)=\delta_1<\kappa\delta_1=\kappa(1-\mathrm{Lip}T_0)$. Hence in infinite dimensions \emph{no exact-oracle probing strategy certifies the global modulus}, and any schedule that freezes its window length from a certified modulus guess can be made to freeze at a wrong rate. \emph{(b) Noise.} With the stochastic oracle, the natural detector (angle statistics: on $R_\varphi$ the increments satisfy $\langle x_j-x_{j-1},Tx_j-Tx_{j-1} \rangle=\cos\varphi\,\norm{x_j-x_{j-1}}\norm{Tx_j-Tx_{j-1}}$ exactly, while on a scalar contraction the angle is $0$) must be estimated from $\widehat T$-responses with per-evaluation noise $\sigma$, and the estimated angle is correlated with the current noise --- precisely the measurability failure of Remark~\ref{rem:discipline}. The conclusion: single-trajectory two-regime optimality, if achievable at all, must be \emph{structural} (residual-feedback updates that need no certified modulus), as in \cite{ParameterFree2026,Diakonikolas2025}, and the stochastic version must route through the ceiling trichotomy of Section~\ref{sec:contraction}. The stochastic two-regime problem itself is settled up to the factor $2$ by parallel composition (Theorem~\ref{thm:stoch-parallel}); in the deterministic layer, Theorem~\ref{thm:oscillatory-escape} removes the overhead entirely via oscillation (Remark~\ref{rem:osc-profile}). What remains open is the single-evaluation-stream \emph{stochastic} version of Theorem~\ref{thm:stoch-parallel}: whether an oscillatory batching scheme --- contraction-phase geometric batches alternating with nonexpansive-phase linear batches on one stream --- removes the factor $2$ there as well.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:oscillatory-bounded-gap}
Let $\theta_n=c_n/(n+2)$ with $c_n\in(0,1]$ and $\liminf c_n=0<\limsup c_n$. Suppose there exist $c''>0$ and $G<\infty$ such that $S=\{k:c_k\ge c''\}$ meets every interval of $G$ consecutive integers. Then for every $\rho$ with $1-\rho\le1/(2G)$ and all $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:osc-bounded-gap}
N_\rho(\varepsilon)\;\ge\;\Bigl(\frac{c''\,\rho D}{16e^{3}\,G\,\varepsilon(1-\rho)}\Bigr)^{1/3}:
\end{equation}
the schedule is polynomially suboptimal on sufficiently sharp contractions. If instead $M(n):=\sum_{j<n}\theta_j=o(\ln n)$ (in particular if all superlevel sets $\{k:c_k\ge c'\}$, $c'>0$, have gaps tending to infinity), then the rotation alternative of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}{\rm(ii)} applies verbatim: the schedule is super-polynomially (or infinitely) slow on rotations.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
For the first claim, let $t=\lceil1/(1-\rho)\rceil\ge2G$ and $N\ge\max(4,2t)$. The window $[N-t,N-1]$ has length $t\ge2G$ and, since $S$ meets every interval of $G$ consecutive integers, it contains at least $\lfloor t/G\rfloor\ge t/(2G)$ points of $S$; for each such point $k$, $\theta_k=c_k/(k+2)\ge c''/(N+1)\ge c''/(2N)$. The total trailing anchor mass is therefore at least
$$\sum_{k\in S\cap[N-t,N-1]}a_k^{(N)}\;\ge\;r_N\sum_{k\in S\cap[N-t,N-1]}\theta_k\;\ge\;r_N\cdot\frac{t}{2G}\cdot\frac{c''}{2N}\;\ge\;\frac{r_Nc''}{4GN(1-\rho)},$$
using $t\ge1/(1-\rho)$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:anchor-floor} (with $r_N\ge(2N)^{-2}$ by \eqref{eq:rn-general}),
$$e_N\;\ge\;D\rho^t\sum_{k=N-t}^{N-1}a_k^{(N)}\;\ge\;\frac{D\rho^t c''}{16GN^3(1-\rho)}\;\ge\;\frac{c''\rho D}{16e^{3}GN^3(1-\rho)},$$
using $\rho^t\ge e^{-3}$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}(i) for $\rho\ge\tfrac12$ (the bound is vacuous otherwise, since $1-\rho>\tfrac12$ forces the right side below $1$ for $\varepsilon\le\varepsilon_0$); $e_N\le\varepsilon$ forces \eqref{eq:osc-bounded-gap}.

For the second claim, the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:dichotomy-general}(ii) never used $c_n\to0$ beyond its consequence $M(n)=o(\ln n)$: if $\sum_n\theta_n<\infty$ the schedule never solves small-angle rotations (case (a)), and if $\sum_n\theta_n=\infty$ then for every $B\ge1$ the $n_B$ construction gives $\varphi_B(\varepsilon)\to0$ with $N_{\varphi_B(\varepsilon)}(\varepsilon)>\varepsilon^{-B}$ (case (b)). Note that when some superlevel set has bounded gaps, $M(n)\ge c''\sum_{k\in S,k\le n}\frac1{k+2}\ge\frac{c''}{G}\sum_{j\le n/G}\frac1j=\Omega(\ln n)$, so the two cases are mutually exclusive and together cover the oscillatory situation except when $M(n)\asymp\ln n$ with all superlevel sets lacunary (Remark~\ref{rem:oscillatory}).
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:master}
Let $(x_n)$ follow \eqref{eq:anchored} with $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable coefficients $\theta_n\in[0,1]$, and let $(\hat x_n)$ be the exact-oracle path with the \emph{same} coefficients: $\hat x_{n+1}=\theta_n x_0+(1-\theta_n)T\hat x_n$, $\hat x_0=x_0$. Set $d_n=x_n-\hat x_n$, $v_n=\mathbb{E}\norm{d_n}^2$. Then for all $n\ge0$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:master}
v_{n+1} \;\le\; (1-\theta_n)^2\, v_n \;+\; (1-\theta_n)^2\,\frac{\sigma^2}{m_n}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, with $W_k^{(n)}:=\prod_{j=k+1}^{n-1}(1-\theta_j)^2$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:master-unfold}
v_N \;\le\; \sigma^2 \sum_{k=0}^{N-1} W_k^{(n)}\,\frac{1}{m_k}.
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Subtracting the two recursions, $d_{n+1} = (1-\theta_n)(Tx_n-T\hat x_n) + (1-\theta_n)\bar\xi_n$. Expand the square:
$$\norm{d_{n+1}}^2 = (1-\theta_n)^2\norm{Tx_n-T\hat x_n}^2 + 2(1-\theta_n)^2\langle Tx_n-T\hat x_n,\bar\xi_n \rangle + (1-\theta_n)^2\norm{\bar\xi_n}^2.$$
Now take conditional expectation given $\mathcal F_{n-1}$. The middle term vanishes: $Tx_n-T\hat x_n$ and $\theta_n$ are $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable, and $\mathbb{E}[\bar\xi_n\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]=0$ (Definition~\ref{def:filtration}), so
$$\mathbb{E}[\langle Tx_n-T\hat x_n,\bar\xi_n \rangle\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}] = \langle Tx_n-T\hat x_n, \mathbb{E}[\bar\xi_n\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}] \rangle = 0.$$
This is the step that fails when $\theta_n$ is correlated with $\bar\xi_n$ (adaptive parameters estimated from the same batch): there the coefficient $(1-\theta_n)^2$ would be $\mathcal F_n$-measurable and could not be pulled out of the conditional expectation. Nonexpansiveness gives $\norm{Tx_n-T\hat x_n}^2\le\norm{d_n}^2$, and $\mathbb{E}[\norm{\bar\xi_n}^2\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]\le\sigma^2/m_n$; taking expectations yields \eqref{eq:master}. Iterating \eqref{eq:master} and using $v_0=0$ gives \eqref{eq:master-unfold}.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\label{rem:comparison}
The path $(\hat x_n)$ is defined by the same coefficients $(\theta_n)$ as the stochastic run, so no independence between coefficients and noise is ever invoked: $\hat x_n$ is a deterministic function of $(\theta_0,\dots,\theta_{n-1})$ given $x_0$, hence $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable. All algorithms of Section~\ref{sec:contraction} use deterministic coefficients, so Lemma~\ref{lem:master} applies with room to spare; the lemma is stated with $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable coefficients only to cover schedules whose coefficients are generated independently of the current batch. The same discipline cannot be maintained for coefficients estimated from the trajectory itself; Section~\ref{sec:contraction} shows what replaces it (deterministic schedules plus a priori modulus information).
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:A1}
Let $X$ be a real Hilbert space or a $2$-uniformly smooth Banach space with smoothness constant $K\ge1$ (Remark~\ref{rem:dualization}), and let $T$ be nonexpansive on $X$ with $\mathrm{Fix}(T)\neq\emptyset$, $D=\norm{x_0-x^*}$, $\varepsilon\in(0,D)$. Run \eqref{eq:anchored} with $\theta_n=1/(n+2)$ and batches $m_k=\lceil c\max\{k,1\}\rceil$ where $c=8K\sigma^2/\varepsilon^2$. Then at $N=\lceil 4D/\varepsilon\rceil+8$ (still $N=\frac{4D}{\varepsilon}(1+o(1))$),
$$\mathbb{E}\,\norm{x_N - Tx_N} \;\le\; \varepsilon, \qquad \sum_{k=1}^{N} m_k \;\le\; 64\,K\,\sigma^2 D^2\,\varepsilon^{-4}\,(1+o(1)).$$
Consequently the minimax oracle complexity of the nonexpansive residual problem on Hilbert spaces and on $2$-uniformly smooth Banach spaces is $O(K\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4})$ --- in particular on $\ell_p$, $L_p$ for $2\le p<\infty$ --- improving the previously available $\tilde O(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-5})$ bound of \cite{BravoContreras2024} on those spaces; subsequently, \cite{Diakonikolas2026b} proved, with high probability, $\tilde O(D\sigma^{2}\varepsilon^{-3}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$ oracle complexity on weak-type-$2$ spaces, which include all $2$-uniformly smooth spaces (see Remark~\ref{rem:A1-position}). The scope is sharp for the recursion-based argument: the master recursion itself fails outside the $2$-uniformly smooth class (Remark~\ref{rem:dualization}), and the $\Omega(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-3})$ lower bound of \cite{BravoContreras2024} lives on $\ell_1$-type spaces, which are not $2$-uniformly smooth; the exponent in arbitrary normed spaces remains open (Section~\ref{sec:open}).
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Subtracting the two recursions, $d_{n+1}=(1-\theta_n)(Tx_n-T\hat x_n)+(1-\theta_n)\bar\xi_n$; expanding the square $\norm{d_{n+1}}^2$ and taking conditional expectation given $\mathcal F_{n-1}$, the cross term vanishes because $Tx_n-T\hat x_n$ and $\theta_n$ are $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable and $\mathbb{E}[\bar\xi_n\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]=0$ (Remark~\ref{rem:discipline}), and nonexpansiveness bounds the first term by $(1-\theta_n)^2\norm{d_n}^2$: this is exactly the computation of Lemma~\ref{lem:master}; in a $2$-uniformly smooth space it yields \eqref{eq:master} with $K\sigma^2$ in place of $\sigma^2$ (Remark~\ref{rem:dualization}). Iterating as in Lemma~\ref{lem:master} with $W_k^{(N)}=\bigl(\tfrac{k+2}{N+1}\bigr)^2$ (telescoping, since $1-\theta_j=\tfrac{j+1}{j+2}$), and separating the $k=0$ term (for which $m_0=\lceil c\rceil\ge c$ and which contributes $O(\sigma^2/(cN^2))=o(1)$),
$$v_N \;\le\; \frac{4K\sigma^2}{c(N+1)^2}+K\sigma^2\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\Bigl(\frac{k+2}{N+1}\Bigr)^2\frac{1}{m_k} \;\le\; \frac{K\sigma^2}{c(N+1)^2}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\frac{(k+2)^2}{k} \;\le\;\frac{K\sigma^2}{2c}\Bigl(1+\frac{16}{N}\Bigr),$$
using $m_k\ge ck$ and $\frac{1}{(N+1)^2}\sum_{k=1}^{N-1}\frac{(k+2)^2}{k} = \frac{1}{(N+1)^2}\bigl(\tfrac{N(N-1)}2+4(N-1)+4H_{N-1}\bigr)\le\tfrac12+\tfrac8N$. With $c=8K\sigma^2/\varepsilon^2$,
$$\sqrt{v_N}\;\le\;\frac{\sqrt{K}\,\sigma}{\sqrt{2c}}\sqrt{1+\tfrac{16}{N}}\;=\;\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\sqrt{1+\tfrac{16}{N}}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\Bigl(1+\frac{8}{N}\Bigr)\;=\;\frac{\varepsilon}{4}+\frac{2\varepsilon}{N}.$$
Then by \eqref{eq:lieder} and the $2$-Lipschitzness of $x\mapsto x-Tx$,
$$\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-Tx_N} \;\le\; \norm{\hat x_N-T\hat x_N} + 2\mathbb{E}\norm{d_N} \;\le\; \frac{2D}{N} + 2\sqrt{v_N} \;\le\; \frac{2D}{N}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+\frac{4\varepsilon}{N}\;=\;\frac{2D+4\varepsilon}{N}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\;\le\;\varepsilon,$$
since $N\ge\frac{4D}{\varepsilon}+8$ gives $\frac{2D+4\varepsilon}{N}\le\frac{(2D+4\varepsilon)\varepsilon}{4D+8\varepsilon}=\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$, and $\mathbb{E}\norm{d_N}\le\sqrt{v_N}$. The query count is $\sum_{k=1}^N \lceil ck\rceil \le cN(N+1)/2 + N = 64K\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4}(1+o(1))$.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
\label{rem:dualization}
The only Hilbert-specific step in Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} is the expansion of $\norm{d_{n+1}}^2$ and the cancellation of the cross term by conditional centering. The correct geometric substitute is \emph{$2$-uniform smoothness}: a Banach space $X$ is $2$-uniformly smooth with constant $K$ if its modulus of smoothness satisfies $\rho_X(\tau):=\sup\{\tfrac12(\norm{x+\tau y}+\norm{x-\tau y})-1:\norm{x}=\norm{y}=1\}\le K\tau^2$ (every Hilbert space qualifies with $K=1$; $\ell_p$, $L_p$ for $2\le p<\infty$ qualify with $K=K(p)$). Such spaces admit a duality mapping $J\colon X\to X^*$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:smoothness}
\norm{x+y}^2\;\le\;\norm{x}^2+2\langle y,Jx \rangle+K\norm{y}^2\qquad\text{for all }x,y\in X,
\end{equation}
and \eqref{eq:smoothness} is in fact an equivalent characterization of $2$-uniform smoothness \cite{XuRoach1991}. Apply \eqref{eq:smoothness} with $x=Tx_n-T\hat x_n$ (which is $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable) and $y=\bar\xi_n$. The pairing term vanishes conditionally: $u:=Jx$ is $\mathcal F_{n-1}$-measurable and $\xi\mapsto\langle \xi,u \rangle$ is a fixed linear functional of the fresh batch, so $\mathbb{E}[\langle \bar\xi_n,u \rangle\mid\mathcal F_{n-1}]=0$; and, applying \eqref{eq:smoothness} successively to the batch sum, $\mathbb{E}\norm{\bar\xi_n}^2\le K\sigma^2/m_n$. Absorbing the second factor $K$ into the first, the master recursion \eqref{eq:master} holds with $K\sigma^2$ in place of $\sigma^2$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} follows verbatim with $c=8K\sigma^2/\varepsilon^2$ and cost $O(K\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4})$; the same substitution covers Proposition~\ref{prop:highprob} (with $c=\lceil16K\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2\vartheta)\rceil$) and the nonexpansive track of Theorem~\ref{thm:stoch-parallel}.

The scope is \emph{sharp for the recursion-based argument}: the centered recursion itself fails outside the $2$-uniformly smooth class, so no patch to the proof can extend it. On $\ell_p$ with $1<p<2$ --- whose dual \emph{is} separable, so a ``separable dual'' formulation does not rescue the claim --- take $T=\mathrm{id}$ (nonexpansive, $\mathrm{Fix}(T)=X$), unit batches, $a=se_1$, and $\xi=\sigma(\varepsilon_1e_1+\varepsilon_2e_2)$ with independent Rademachers $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2$. Then
$$\mathbb{E}\norm{a+\xi}^2\;=\;\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(|s+\sigma\varepsilon_1|^p+\sigma^p\bigr)^{2/p}\Bigr]\;\ge\;\Bigl(s^p+\sigma^p+\tfrac{p(p-1)}2\,s^{p-2}\sigma^2\Bigr)^{2/p}\;=\;s^2+\sigma^2+\tfrac2p\,s^{2-p}\sigma^p\bigl(1+o(1)\bigr),$$
where the inequality is Jensen applied to the convex function $u\mapsto(u+\sigma^p)^{2/p}$ together with the convexity bound $\tfrac12(|s+\sigma|^p+|s-\sigma|^p)\ge s^p+\tfrac{p(p-1)}2s^{p-2}\sigma^2$ (Taylor's theorem, $p>1$), and the expansion uses $(1+u)^{2/p}=1+\tfrac2p u+o(u)$ as $\sigma/s\to0$ (the coefficient of $\sigma^2$ in the intermediate expression is $p-1<1$, and the deficit $(2-p)\sigma^2$ is absorbed by $\tfrac2p s^{2-p}\sigma^p$ already for $s\ge\sigma$, since $\tfrac2p\ge2-p$). The excess $\tfrac2p s^{2-p}\sigma^p$ over $\norm{a}^2+\mathbb{E}\norm{\xi}^2=s^2+\sigma^2$ dominates $\sigma^2$ whenever $s\gg\sigma$; with batch size $m$ the excess scales as $s^{2-p}\mathbb{E}|\bar\xi_1|^p\asymp s^{2-p}\sigma^p m^{-p/2}$, which exceeds $K\sigma^2/m$ for any fixed $K$ as $m\to\infty$. Hence no fixed constant repairs the master recursion on $\ell_p$, $1<p<2$. The lower-bound instances of \cite{BravoContreras2024} are $\ell_1$-type, which are not $2$-uniformly smooth either; the exponent in genuinely arbitrary normed spaces remains open (Section~\ref{sec:open}).
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:highprob}
Assume $D\ge4\varepsilon$ (so $N=\lceil4D/\varepsilon\rceil+8\ge24$; for smaller $N$ enlarge the additive constant). Let the oracle noise be Gaussian (as permitted by Definition~\ref{def:oracle}) or, more generally, sub-Gaussian with parameter $\sigma^2$ in each direction: $\mathbb{E}[e^{\lambda\langle \xi,u \rangle}\mid x]\le e^{\lambda^2\sigma^2/2}$ for every unit vector $u$. Then in the setting of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1}, with failure parameter $\vartheta\in(0,1)$ and $c=\lceil 16K\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2\vartheta)\rceil$,
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{x_N-Tx_N}\le\varepsilon\bigr)\;\ge\;1-\vartheta .$$
(Only second moments are used; the argument is valid on any $2$-uniformly smooth space with $K$ absorbed into $c$, and the sub-Gaussian concentration's $\ln(1/\vartheta)$ is traded for Markov's $\vartheta^{-1}$, leaving the complexity exponent unchanged.)
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Let $d_n=x_n-\hat x_n$ as in Lemma~\ref{lem:master}. The pathwise recursion $\norm{d_{n+1}}\le(1-\theta_n)\norm{d_n}+(1-\theta_n)\norm{\bar\xi_n}$ (from $\norm{Tx_n-T\hat x_n}\le\norm{d_n}$, no expectation) unfolds to
\begin{equation}\label{eq:pathwise}
\norm{d_N}\;\le\;\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}\alpha_k\norm{\bar\xi_k},\qquad \alpha_k:=(1-\theta_k)\prod_{j=k+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)=\frac{k+1}{N+1},
\end{equation}
exactly the computation of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} with $m_k=\lceil ck\rceil$ (which uses only independence of the fresh batches, Definition~\ref{def:oracle}, and $\mathbb{E}\norm{\bar\xi_k}^2\le\sigma^2/m_k$) gives, for the same variance bound $v_N$,
$$\mathbb{E}\norm{d_N}^2\;\le\;v_N\;\le\;\frac{K\sigma^2}{2c}\Bigl(1+\frac{16}{N}\Bigr)\;\le\;\frac{K\sigma^2}{c}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon^2\vartheta}{16}\;\le\;\frac{\varepsilon^2}{16}.$$
Markov's inequality applied to $\norm{d_N}^2$ now yields
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{d_N}>\varepsilon/4\bigr)\;=\;\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{d_N}^2>\varepsilon^2/16\bigr)\;\le\;\frac{16\,v_N}{\varepsilon^2}\;\le\;\frac{16K\sigma^2}{c\,\varepsilon^2}\;\le\;\vartheta,
\qquad c=\Bigl\lceil\frac{16K\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2\vartheta}\Bigr\rceil.$$
(The sharper $\ln(1/\vartheta)$ dependence can be recovered from \eqref{eq:pathwise} by concentration of sub-Gaussian sums \cite{Pinelis1994}, at the price of Hilbert-space arguments; we do not pursue it since the complexity exponent is unaffected.)
Finally $\norm{x_N-Tx_N}\le\norm{\hat x_N-T\hat x_N}+2\norm{d_N}\le\varepsilon/2+\varepsilon/2=\varepsilon$ by \eqref{eq:lieder}, with probability at least $1-\vartheta$.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:master-contract}
In the setting of Lemma~\ref{lem:master}, assume additionally that $T$ is a $\gamma$-contraction, and write $\delta=1-\gamma$. Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:master-contract}
v_{n+1}\;\le\;(1-\theta_n)^2\gamma^2\,v_n\;+\;(1-\theta_n)^2\,\frac{\sigma^2}{m_n}.
\end{equation}
Consequently, for constant coefficients $\theta_n\equiv\theta\in[0,\tfrac12]$ and $m_n\equiv m$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stationary}
v_N\;\le\;\gamma^{2N}D^2\;+\;\frac{\sigma^2}{m}\cdot\frac{(1-\theta)^2}{1-(1-\theta)^2\gamma^2},\qquad\text{and}\qquad 1-(1-\theta)^2\gamma^2\;\ge\;\theta+\delta/2 .
\end{equation}
In particular $v_N\le\gamma^{2N}D^2+\sigma^2/(m(\theta+\delta/2))$: the stationary variance floor of Section~\ref{sec:contraction}. (Recall $v_0=0$ for the comparison path; the displayed $\gamma^{2N}D^2$ term uses the crude $v_0\le D^2$ and is what is needed when the recursion is applied to $w_n=\mathbb{E}\norm{x_n-x^*}^2$ directly.)
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Identical to Lemma~\ref{lem:master}, using $\norm{Tx_n-T\hat x_n}\le\gamma\norm{d_n}$ in place of nonexpansiveness; the cross term still vanishes by conditional unbiasedness. For \eqref{eq:stationary}, iterate \eqref{eq:master-contract} and use $(1-\theta)^2\gamma^2\le(1-\theta)(1-\delta)$, so $1-(1-\theta)^2\gamma^2\ge1-(1-\theta)(1-\delta)\ge\theta+\delta-\theta\delta\ge\theta+\delta/2$ for $\theta\le\tfrac12$, $\delta\le1$.
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- remark-19: body_md -->
\label{rem:unification}
Lemma~\ref{lem:master-contract} reproduces the contraction-regime noise floor from the same envelope: for a $\gamma$-contraction and a schedule with $\theta\ll\delta$, the envelope is $W_k^{(N)}\approx\gamma^{2(N-k)}$, and with constant batch $m$, $v_N\le\frac{\sigma^2}{m}\sum_{j\ge0}\gamma^{2j}=\frac{\sigma^2}{m(1-\gamma^2)}$. The contrast with the nonexpansive envelope $\bigl(\tfrac{k+2}{N+1}\bigr)^2$ is the entire content of Q2 versus Q3: geometric envelopes admit constant batches; polynomial envelopes require the linear allocation $m_k\propto k$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1}.
<!-- end remark-19 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:monotone-reduction}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hilbert space. For every nonexpansive $T$ on $\mathcal{H}$, the operator $F=I-T$ is monotone and $2$-Lipschitz, with $\operatorname{zer}(F)=\mathrm{Fix}(T)$ and $\norm{F(x)}=\norm{x-Tx}$; the single-point oracle for $T$ induces an unbiased single-point oracle for $F$ with the same variance bound $\sigma^2$. Consequently the residual problem of Section~\ref{sec:master} is a stochastic monotone inclusion with $(L,D)=(2,D)$, and:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item the algorithm RAIN of \cite[Theorem 4.2]{ChenLuo2024} reaches $\mathbb{E}\norm{x-Tx}\le\varepsilon$ with $\tilde O(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$ oracle evaluations;
\item any algorithm reaching $\mathbb{E}\norm{x-Tx}\le\varepsilon$ on the subclass $\{T=I-\nabla f:\ f\ \text{convex},\ 2\text{-smooth}\}$ requires $\tilde\Omega(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$ evaluations, by \cite[Theorem 6.2]{ChenLuo2024} with $L=2$ (the hard instances are gradient maps, and $T=I-\nabla f$ is nonexpansive for $2$-smooth convex $f$ by Baillon--Haddad co-coercivity; the lower bound for the subclass lower-bounds the general class since the subclass is contained in it).
\end{enumerate}
Hence the minimax oracle complexity of the general nonexpansive residual problem on Hilbert spaces is $\tilde\Theta(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- remark-20: body_md -->
\label{rem:chenluo-position}
Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone-reduction} is a \emph{reduction} to \cite{ChenLuo2024}, not a new algorithm or a new lower bound; we state it because it settles the Hilbert exponent (open after \cite{BravoContreras2024}) and because the verification of the hypotheses (monotonicity and $2$-Lipschitzness of $F=I-T$ for \emph{every} nonexpansive $T$, including non-gradient ones; embedding of their hard family via Baillon--Haddad) is short but not entirely automatic. We checked \cite{ChenLuo2024} against the published version (JMLR \textbf{25}(387):1--44, 2024): their Theorem 4.2 states that RAIN, run with regularization parameter $\lambda=\min\{\varepsilon/D,L\}$, outputs a $3\varepsilon$-stationary point in expectation at SFO complexity $\tilde O(LD/\varepsilon+\sigma^2/\varepsilon^2)$; running it with target $\varepsilon/3$ yields item (i) at the same complexity up to constants. Their Theorem 6.2 is the matching convex-concave lower bound, proved with the separable hard family $H(x,y)=F(x)-F(y)$ whose gradient map is monotone and $L$-Lipschitz. Their Epoch-SEG analysis uses only monotonicity and $L$-Lipschitzness of the operator (and strong monotonicity of the regularized operator), so it applies verbatim to $F=I-T$, which is monotone and $2$-Lipschitz but need not be a gradient field.
<!-- end remark-20 -->
<!-- remark-21: body_md -->
\label{rem:A1-position}
Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone-reduction} supersedes the \emph{rate} of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} on Hilbert spaces: $\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}+D\varepsilon^{-1}\ll\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4}$ for $\varepsilon\ll D$. Subsequently, Diakonikolas, Guzm\'an, and Mart\'inez-Rubio \cite{Diakonikolas2026b} proved, for the same single-point bounded-variance oracle, a high-probability bound $N=\tilde O\bigl(\tfrac{D}{\varepsilon}\bigl(1+\bigl(C\log\tfrac{D}{\varepsilon}\cdot L\mathfrak{T}\sigma/\varepsilon\bigr)^{2}\bigr)\log\tfrac1\delta\bigr)$ on every space of equal-norm Rademacher type $2$ with constant $\mathfrak{T}$ and $L$-Lipschitz retraction (their Theorem~5 with $q=2$); $2$-uniformly smooth spaces qualify with $\mathfrak{T}=O(\sqrt K)$ and $L\le2$. This is $\tilde O(D\sigma^{2}\varepsilon^{-3}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$ up to polylogarithms, and its ratio to the bound of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} is $\varepsilon/(KD)$ --- so it supersedes the rate of Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} whenever $\varepsilon\lesssim KD$, in particular throughout the regime $\varepsilon\le D/4$ treated here. Their lower bound $\Omega(D\varepsilon^{-1}+D\sigma^{2}\varepsilon^{-3})$ for $\ell_\infty$-instances (their Theorem~13) is nearly tight against their upper bound and holds against arbitrary randomized algorithms, strengthening the linear-span $\Omega(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-3})$ bound of \cite{BravoContreras2024}. Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} remains the contribution on Q3 for three reasons: it is a self-contained single-loop analysis using only anchoring and batching (no extragradient steps, no recursive regularization, no monotonicity machinery), which is the analysis that also generates the contraction-regime noise floor of Section~\ref{sec:contraction}; it extends beyond Hilbert spaces to every $2$-uniformly smooth Banach space (Remark~\ref{rem:dualization}), where, at the time of writing, it improved the state of the art from $\tilde O(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-5})$ to $O(K\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4})$ --- the improvement over the $D$-independent bound of \cite{BravoContreras2024} holding in the regime $\varepsilon\lesssim D^{-2}$, with the two bounds complementary in the opposite regime and a $D$-independent $O(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-4})$ bound on $2$-uniformly smooth spaces open; and its proof (dualization + deterministic envelopes) is the template for the stochastic adaptive question of Remark~\ref{rem:detection}.
<!-- end remark-21 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:affine-lower}
Fix $\gamma\in(0,1)$, $\sigma>0$, $\varepsilon\in(0,D/2)$, and $\vartheta\in(0,1/2)$ (throughout, $\vartheta$ denotes a failure probability, in contrast to the contraction gap $\delta=1-\gamma$). Any algorithm that, using any adaptive querying strategy with single-point bounded-variance oracle evaluations (Definition~\ref{def:oracle}), outputs $\hat x$ with
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{\hat x - x^*}\le \varepsilon\bigr)\;\ge\;1-\vartheta \quad\text{for every } T\in\mathcal{C}_\gamma,$$
requires at least
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-lb}
M \;\ge\; (1-2\vartheta)^2\,\frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2}
\end{equation}
oracle samples on the affine subfamily $T_{\gamma,z}(x)=\gamma x+(1-\gamma)z$, $z\in[-D,D]$.
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Consider the two instances $\theta_0=(\gamma,z-\varepsilon)$ and $\theta_1=(\gamma,z+\varepsilon)$, both in $\mathcal{C}_\gamma$ (same contraction factor; fixed points $z\mp\varepsilon$ at distance $2\varepsilon$). For every query point $x$ the response means differ by
$$\Delta(x) = T_{\gamma,z+\varepsilon}(x)-T_{\gamma,z-\varepsilon}(x) = (1-\gamma)\cdot 2\varepsilon,$$
independent of $x$. Condition on any realization of the algorithm's query points (adaptivity is allowed; the argument is pointwise in the transcript): with Gaussian noise of variance $\sigma^2$ per evaluation, a batch of $k$ evaluations contributes KL divergence $k\,\norm{\Delta}^2/(2\sigma^2) = 2k(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2/\sigma^2$, and by the chain rule the total KL divergence after $M$ samples is at most $2M(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2/\sigma^2$, regardless of the (adaptive, data-dependent) query placement. Le\,Cam's two-point bound \cite[Theorem 2.2]{Tsybakov2009} then gives
$$\inf_{\hat x}\ \max_{i\in\{0,1\}}\ \mathbb{P}_{\theta_i}\bigl(\norm{\hat x-x^*(\theta_i)}\ge\varepsilon\bigr) \;\ge\; \frac12 - \frac12\sqrt{\tfrac12\,\mathrm{KL}} \;\ge\; \frac12 - \sqrt{\frac{M(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2}{4\sigma^2}}.$$
A $1-\vartheta$ accurate algorithm must make the left side $\le\vartheta$, forcing $\sqrt{M(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2/(4\sigma^2)}\ge\tfrac12-\vartheta=\tfrac{1-2\vartheta}{2}$, i.e.\ \eqref{eq:affine-lb}.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- remark-22: body_md -->
Three features distinguish Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-lower} from resistant-oracle lower bounds \cite{BravoContreras2024}: (i) it is \emph{parametric} --- two affine instances; (ii) the mean gap is $x$-independent, so adaptivity and query placement are irrelevant; (iii) it applies to arbitrary algorithms with memory, not only span-class iterations. The price is scope: the bound is specific to contractions (the affine family is not nonexpansive-hard), which is exactly what Q2 needs. The bound is stated at constant confidence; its high-probability refinement is Lemma~\ref{lem:hp-lb}.
<!-- end remark-22 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{thm:affine-upper}
Let $T(x)=\gamma x+(1-\gamma)z$ on $\mathbb{R}$ with $\gamma\in(0,1)$, $z\in[-D,D]$ unknown (the construction is one-dimensional --- both probes lie on a line; in a Hilbert space the same probes identify $\langle z,u\rangle$ for the probe direction $u$, but the component of $z$ orthogonal to the probe line is unobservable, so the guarantee $\norm{\hat z-z}\le\varepsilon$ requires $z$ to lie on the probe line, otherwise the bound carries an additional $\norm{z_\perp}$ term). The estimator querying $m/2$ evaluations at $0$ and $m/2$ at $D$ and returning
$$\hat\gamma = \frac{\bar y_D-\bar y_0}{D}, \qquad \hat z = \frac{\bar y_0}{1-\hat\gamma},$$
satisfies $\mathbb{P}(\norm{\hat z-z}\le\varepsilon)\ge2/3$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:affine-ub}
m \;=\; O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2}\Bigr),
\end{equation}
where the sufficient explicit threshold is $m=\lceil480\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2)\rceil$ (constant not optimized; Remark~\ref{rem:constants} applies).
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Write $b=(1-\gamma)z$, $\bar y_0 = b+\zeta_0$, $\bar y_D = \gamma D+b+\zeta_D$ with independent $\zeta_0,\zeta_D$ of variance $\le 2\sigma^2/m$ each, and set
$$\beta\;:=\;\frac{b}{D(1-\gamma)}\;\in\;[-1,1]\qquad(\text{since }\abs{b}=(1-\gamma)\abs{z}\le(1-\gamma)D).$$
Then $\eta:=\hat\gamma-\gamma = (\zeta_D-\zeta_0)/D$ with $\mathbb{E}\eta^2\le4\sigma^2/(mD^2)$, and linearising $1/(1-\hat\gamma)$ about $1/(1-\gamma)$,
$$\hat z - z \;=\; \frac{\zeta_0}{1-\gamma}+\frac{b\,\eta}{(1-\gamma)^2}+R \;=\; \frac{\zeta_0(1-\beta)+\zeta_D\,\beta}{1-\gamma}+R,$$
where the remainder collects exactly the terms quadratic in the noise: with $E_0:=\{|\eta|\le(1-\gamma)/2\}$ (so that $1-\hat\gamma\ge(1-\gamma)/2$ on $E_0$),
$$R\;=\;\underbrace{b\,\eta\Bigl(\frac{1}{(1-\gamma-\eta)(1-\gamma)}-\frac{1}{(1-\gamma)^2}\Bigr)}_{R_1}\;+\;\underbrace{\zeta_0\,\eta\,\frac{1}{(1-\gamma-\eta)(1-\gamma)}}_{R_2},$$
and therefore, on $E_0$,
$$|R_1|\;\le\;\frac{2|b|\,\eta^2}{(1-\gamma)^3}\;\le\;\frac{2D\,\eta^2}{(1-\gamma)^2},\qquad |R_2|\;\le\;\frac{2|\zeta_0|\,|\eta|}{(1-\gamma)^2}.$$
We control $R$ in expectation rather than pointwise (the product $|\zeta_0|\,|\eta|$ is only first-order in each noise factor, so a pointwise bound on a high-probability event costs unnecessary constants): by Cauchy--Schwarz, $\mathbb{E}|\zeta_0\eta|\le\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\zeta_0^2\,\mathbb{E}\eta^2}\le\sqrt{(2\sigma^2/m)(4\sigma^2/(mD^2))}=2\sqrt2\,\sigma^2/(mD)$, hence
$$\mathbb{E}\bigl[|R|\,\mathbf 1_{E_0}\bigr]\;\le\;\frac{2D\,\mathbb{E}\eta^2}{(1-\gamma)^2}+\frac{2\,\mathbb{E}|\zeta_0\eta|}{(1-\gamma)^2}\;\le\;\frac{(8+4\sqrt2)\,\sigma^2}{mD(1-\gamma)^2}\;\le\;\frac{14\,\sigma^2}{mD(1-\gamma)^2}.$$

We bound the failure probability $\mathbb{P}(|\hat z-z|>\varepsilon)$ by the sum of three terms.\
\emph{Term 1: $E_0^c$.} $\eta$ is centered Gaussian with variance parameter $\le4\sigma^2/(mD^2)$, so $\mathbb{P}(E_0^c)\le2\exp\bigl(-(1-\gamma)^2mD^2/(32\sigma^2)\bigr)\le2e^{-4}\le1/12$ once $m\ge128\sigma^2/(D^2(1-\gamma)^2)$, which is implied by $m\ge480\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2)$ since $\varepsilon\le D/2$ gives $480/\varepsilon^2\ge480\cdot4/D^2\ge128/D^2$.\
\emph{Term 2: the linear part.} Its variance is
$$\operatorname{Var}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta_0(1-\beta)+\zeta_D\,\beta}{1-\gamma}\Bigr)=\frac{(1-\beta)^2\operatorname{Var}\zeta_0+\beta^2\operatorname{Var}\zeta_D}{(1-\gamma)^2}\;\le\;\frac{2\sigma^2}{m(1-\gamma)^2}\bigl((1-\beta)^2+\beta^2\bigr)\;\le\;\frac{10\sigma^2}{m(1-\gamma)^2},$$
the last step using $\max_{\beta\in[-1,1]}\bigl((1-\beta)^2+\beta^2\bigr)=5$ (attained at $\beta=-1$). Note that the two displayed terms are correlated through $\zeta_0$ (via $\hat\gamma$); the computation above accounts for this exactly --- dropping the covariance, as a naive term-by-term estimate would do, gives the smaller but incorrect constant $6$. By Chebyshev, $\mathbb{P}(|\mathrm{linear}|>\varepsilon/2)\le40\sigma^2/(m(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2)\le1/12$ for $m\ge480\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2)$.\
\emph{Term 3: the remainder.} By Markov's inequality, $\mathbb{P}(|R|>\varepsilon/4)\le\mathbb{E}[|R|\mathbf 1_{E_0}]/\tfrac{\varepsilon}{4}+\mathbb{P}(E_0^c)\le56\sigma^2/(mD\varepsilon(1-\gamma)^2)+1/12\le1/6$ for $m\ge480\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2(1-\gamma)^2)$, using $480D\ge672\varepsilon$ (i.e.\ $\varepsilon\le5D/7$), which holds since $\varepsilon\le D/2$.

Summing the three bounds, $\mathbb{P}(|\hat z-z|\le\varepsilon)\ge1-1/12-1/12-1/6=2/3$ with $m$ as in \eqref{eq:affine-ub}.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:affine-theta}
For the affine contraction class, the minimax oracle complexity of reaching $\varepsilon$-accuracy is $\Theta\!\bigl(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}(1-\gamma)^{-2}\bigr)$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{thm:geobatch}
Let $T$ be a $\gamma$-contraction with $\gamma$ \emph{known}, $\delta=1-\gamma$, $D=\norm{x_0-x^*}\ge\varepsilon$. Set $\bar\gamma=\max\{\gamma,\tfrac12\}$, $\bar\delta=1-\bar\gamma$,
$$T=\Bigl\lceil\frac{\ln(2D/\varepsilon)}{\ln(1/\bar\gamma)}\Bigr\rceil,\qquad m_k=\Bigl\lceil\frac{2\sigma^2\,\bar\gamma^{\,T-k}}{\bar\delta\,\varepsilon^2}\Bigr\rceil .$$
Then the Krasnoselskii--Mann iteration $x_{k+1}=\widehat T_{m_k}(x_k)$ satisfies
$$\mathbb{E}\norm{x_T-x^*}^2\;\le\;2\varepsilon^2,\qquad \sum_{k=0}^{T-1}m_k\;=\;O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2}{\delta^2\varepsilon^2}+\frac{\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\delta}\Bigr).$$
Only an upper bound on $\gamma$ is needed; see Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling}.
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Apply the squared-norm recursion to $w_k:=\mathbb{E}\norm{x_k-x^*}^2$ directly (this avoids the factor-$2$ comparison step and is the honest bookkeeping): $x_{k+1}-x^*=(Tx_k-x^*)+\bar\xi_k$, the cross term vanishes in expectation because $\bar\xi_k$ is a fresh batch ($\mathbb{E}[\bar\xi_k\mid\mathcal F_{k-1}]=0$ and $Tx_k-x^*$ is $\mathcal F_{k-1}$-measurable), and $\norm{Tx_k-x^*}\le\gamma\norm{x_k-x^*}$. Hence $w_{k+1}\le\gamma^2w_k+\sigma^2/m_k\le\bar\gamma^2w_k+\sigma^2/m_k$ (since $\gamma\le\bar\gamma$); unrolling with $w_0\le D^2$,
$$w_T\;\le\;\bar\gamma^{2T}D^2\;+\;\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\bar\gamma^{2(T-1-k)}\frac{\sigma^2}{m_k}.$$
The first term is at most $\varepsilon^2/4$ by the choice of $T$ ($\bar\gamma^T D\le\varepsilon/2$). For the second term, $\sigma^2/m_k\le\frac{\bar\delta\varepsilon^2}{2}\bar\gamma^{-(T-k)}$, so the $k$-th summand is at most $\frac{\bar\delta\varepsilon^2}{2}\bar\gamma^{T-2-k}$; summing the geometric series yields at most
$$\frac{\bar\delta\varepsilon^2}{2}\cdot\frac{\bar\gamma^{-1}}{1-\bar\gamma}\;=\;\frac{\varepsilon^2}{2\bar\gamma}\;\le\;\varepsilon^2,$$
using $\bar\gamma\ge\tfrac12$. Altogether $w_T\le\varepsilon^2/4+\varepsilon^2=\tfrac54\varepsilon^2\le2\varepsilon^2$, as claimed. The batch schedule is geometrically increasing, and since $\bar\delta\ge\delta$,
$$\sum_k m_k\;\le\;\frac{2\sigma^2}{\bar\delta\varepsilon^2}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\bar\gamma}+T\;=\;O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2}{\delta^2\varepsilon^2}+\frac{\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\delta}\Bigr),$$
as $T=O(\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta)$ for all $\gamma\in(0,1)$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:hp-lb}
Fix $\gamma\in(0,1)$, $\vartheta\in(0,1/8)$, $\varepsilon\in(0,D/2)$. On the affine subfamily $T_{\gamma,z}(x)=\gamma x+(1-\gamma)z$, $z\in\{-D,D\}$ shifted so that the two fixed points are at distance $2\varepsilon$, any algorithm whose output satisfies $\mathbb{P}(\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon)\ge1-\vartheta$ under both instances requires
$$M\;\ge\;\frac{\sigma^2\,\ln(1/(8\vartheta))}{(1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2}$$
oracle samples, even with adaptive querying and full knowledge of $\gamma$.
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Let the two instances be $z=\pm\varepsilon$ (fixed points $\pm\varepsilon$, distance $2\varepsilon$), same $\gamma$, Gaussian noise. If $\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon$ with probability $\ge1-\vartheta$ under both, then the test $\Psi=+$ iff $\hat x>0$ discriminates with error $\le\vartheta$ under both. For the Gaussian location structure, the log-likelihood ratio of the two hypotheses has a clean form: for any query transcript, each response $r_i=T_{\pm}(q_i)+\xi_i$ can be reduced to the sufficient scalar $w_i=(r_i-\gamma q_i)/(1-\gamma)=z+\xi_i/(1-\gamma)$, so the full transcript is equivalent (in law) to $M$ i.i.d.\ observations $w_i=z+\eta_i$ with $\eta_i\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2/(1-\gamma)^2)$. The log-likelihood ratio is therefore
$$\log\frac{d\mathbb{P}_{+}}{d\mathbb{P}_{-}}=\frac{2\varepsilon}{\tau^2}\sum_{i=1}^M(w_i-\bar w\cdot 0)=\frac{2\varepsilon}{\tau^2}\sum_{i=1}^M w_i\;\sim\;\mathcal N\Bigl(\pm\frac{2\varepsilon^2M}{\tau^2},\,\frac{4\varepsilon^2M}{\tau^2}\Bigr),\qquad \tau^2=\frac{\sigma^2}{(1-\gamma)^2},$$
under $\mathbb{P}_{\pm}$. Writing $x=\varepsilon\sqrt{M}/\tau$, the Neyman--Pearson lemma gives minimax test error $\Phi(-x)$, and if $x<1$ this error is at least $\Phi(-1)>\tfrac18>\vartheta$, contradicting the assumption; hence $x\ge1$ and the standard bound $\Phi(-x)\ge\tfrac18 e^{-x^2}$ for $x\ge1$ (from $\Phi(-x)\ge\varphi(x)/(2x)$ and $\varphi(x)/(2x)\ge\tfrac18 e^{-x^2}$ for $x\ge1$) shows that error $\le\vartheta$ forces $e^{-x^2}\le8\vartheta$, i.e.\ $x^2\ge\ln(1/(8\vartheta))$, i.e.\ $M\ge\tau^2\ln(1/(8\vartheta))/\varepsilon^2=\sigma^2\ln(1/(8\vartheta))/((1-\gamma)^2\varepsilon^2)$. Adaptive query placement is irrelevant because the per-sample information about $z$ is the same at every query point.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{thm:hp}
Assume the oracle noise is $\sigma$-sub-Gaussian in each direction ($\mathbb{E} e^{\langle u,\xi\rangle}\le e^{\sigma^2\norm{u}^2/2}$ for every unit $u$; Gaussian noise, as permitted by Definition~\ref{def:oracle}, qualifies), with $\gamma$ known. Then the geometric schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch} with all batches enlarged by $\Lambda=\lceil 16\ln(2/\vartheta)\rceil$ satisfies
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{x_T-x^*}\le\varepsilon\bigr)\;\ge\;1-\vartheta,\qquad \sum_k m_k = O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2\ln(1/\vartheta)}{\delta_{\gamma}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\ln(1/\vartheta)\,\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\delta_{\gamma}}\Bigr),$$
where $\delta_\gamma=1-\gamma$. This is tight: on the affine subfamily with Gaussian noise, success probability $1-\vartheta$ forces $\Omega\bigl(\sigma^{2}\delta_\gamma^{-2}\varepsilon^{-2}\ln(1/\vartheta)\bigr)$ evaluations by Lemma~\ref{lem:hp-lb}.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Write $x_T-x^*=\gamma^{T}(\hat x_0-x^*)+d_T$ with $d_T$ the stochastic deviation of the path from its exact orbit. Pathwise contraction gives $\norm{d_T}\le\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\bar\gamma^{T-1-k}\norm{\bar\xi_k}=:\tilde S$. Each $\norm{\bar\xi_k}$ is sub-Gaussian with parameter $\sigma^2/m_k$, so $\tilde S$, a sum of independent sub-Gaussians, is sub-Gaussian with parameter $\sigma^2\sum_k\bar\gamma^{2(T-1-k)}/m_k$. With $m_k=\Lambda\bar m_k$ and the schedule $\{\bar m_k\}$ of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch}, the proof of that theorem gives $\sum_k\bar\gamma^{2(T-1-k)}\sigma^2/\bar m_k\le\varepsilon^2$, hence $\tilde S$ has parameter at most $\varepsilon^2/\Lambda$ and
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\tilde S>\varepsilon/2\bigr)\;\le\;\exp\Bigl(-\frac{(\varepsilon/2)^2}{2\varepsilon^2/\Lambda}\Bigr)\;=\;\exp(-\Lambda/8)\;\le\;\vartheta.$$
Since $\bar\gamma^T D\le\varepsilon/2$ by the choice of $T$, $\norm{x_T-x^*}\le\varepsilon$ with probability $1-\vartheta$, at $\Lambda$ times the query cost of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-15: body_md -->
\label{thm:hp-bounded}
Let $T$ be a $\gamma$-contraction with $\gamma$ \emph{known}, $\vartheta\in(0,\tfrac12)$, $D\ge\varepsilon$, and suppose only the bounded-variance oracle of Definition~\ref{def:oracle}. Run $B=\bigl\lceil\frac{32}{9}\ln(2/\vartheta)\bigr\rceil$ independent replicates of plain Krasnoselskii--Mann, each for $T=\bigl\lceil\ln(2D/\varepsilon)/\ln(1/\bar\gamma)\bigr\rceil$ steps with per-step batch $s=\bigl\lceil288\,\sigma^2/(\bar\delta\varepsilon^2)\bigr\rceil$, and output the geometric median $\hat x$ of the $B$ terminal iterates. Then
$$\mathbb{P}\bigl(\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon\bigr)\;\ge\;1-\vartheta,\qquad\text{total evaluations}\;=\;O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2\,\ln(1/\vartheta)\,\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\bar\delta^{2}\,\varepsilon^{2}}\Bigr).$$
Conversely, for Gaussian oracles the affine subfamily forces $\Omega\bigl(\sigma^2\bar\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-2}\ln(1/\vartheta)\bigr)$ evaluations (Lemma~\ref{lem:hp-lb}) and the noiseless subclass forces $\Omega(\bar\delta^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon))$ evaluations for success probability $1-\vartheta$ among anchored and Krasnoselskii--Mann schemes (the class of \cite{ParkRyu2022}; Remark~\ref{rem:span-class}); since one algorithm must handle both, the bounded-variance high-probability complexity with known modulus is $\Theta\bigl(\sigma^2\bar\delta^{-2}\varepsilon^{-2}\ln(1/\vartheta)+\bar\delta^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon)\bigr)$ within the anchored/Krasnoselskii--Mann class up to a factor $\ln(D/\varepsilon)$ in the stochastic term (the upper bound carries $\ln(D/\varepsilon)$ in the first term where the lower bound does not; closing this logarithm for bounded-variance noise is open).
<!-- end theorem-15 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Let $\tilde x^{(b)}$ be the terminal iterate of replicate $b$ and $\hat x_T$ the exact-orbit point (identical across replicates), and write $d^{(b)}_T=\tilde x^{(b)}_T-\hat x_T$. By Lemma~\ref{lem:master-contract} with $\theta=0$ (the comparison path is the exact Krasnoselskii--Mann orbit and $v_0=0$; no independence between coefficients and noise is used, cf.\ Remark~\ref{rem:discipline}),
$$\mathbb{E}\bigl\|d^{(b)}_T\bigr\|^2\;\le\;\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\gamma^{2(T-1-k)}\,\frac{\sigma^2}{s}\;\le\;\frac{\sigma^2}{s(1-\gamma^2)}\;\le\;\frac{\sigma^2}{s\bar\delta},$$
using $1-\gamma^2=(1-\gamma)(1+\gamma)\ge\bar\delta$. (We stress that $d^{(b)}_T$ is \emph{not} decomposed into a mean of independent per-evaluation noises: for nonlinear $T$ the operators governing the deviation depend on the whole trajectory of the replicate, so such noises are not independent; the master-lemma recursion is the correct tool and requires none of that independence.) Hence, by Chebyshev, each replicate satisfies
$$\mathbb{P}\Big(\Big\|d^{(b)}_T\Big\|>\frac{\varepsilon}{6}\Big)\;\le\;\frac{36\,\mathbb{E}\big\|d^{(b)}_T\big\|^2}{\varepsilon^2}\;\le\;\frac{36\sigma^2}{s\,\bar\delta\,\varepsilon^2}\;\le\;\frac18$$
by the choice of $s$. The replicates are independent, so Hoeffding's inequality bounds the number $F$ of failing replicates by
$$\mathbb{P}\Bigl(F\ge\frac{B}{2}\Bigr)\;=\;\mathbb{P}\Bigl(F-\frac{B}{8}\ge\frac{3B}{8}\Bigr)\;\le\;\exp\Bigl(-\frac{9B}{32}\Bigr)\;\le\;\frac{\vartheta}{2}.$$
On the complementary event, strictly more than half of the replicates satisfy $\|d^{(b)}_T\|\le\varepsilon/6$, and the standard breakdown property of the geometric median (a strict majority in a ball of radius $r$ forces the median within $3r$; see \cite{Minsker2015}) gives $\|\hat x-\hat x_T\|\le3\cdot\varepsilon/6=\varepsilon/2$. Finally $\norm{\hat x_T-x^*}\le\bar\gamma^T D\le\varepsilon/2$ by the choice of $T$, so $\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon$ with probability at least $1-\vartheta$, at total cost $B\,T\,s$ as displayed.

For the lower bounds, the affine subfamily with Gaussian noise forces $\Omega\bigl(\sigma^2\ln(1/\vartheta)/(\bar\delta^2\varepsilon^2)\bigr)$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:hp-lb} (valid against adaptive algorithms with full modulus knowledge), and the noiseless subclass forces $\Omega(\bar\delta^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon))$ for success probability $1-\vartheta$ among anchored and Krasnoselskii--Mann schemes, since for any such scheme $\norm{x_n-x^*}\ge\gamma^nD$ holds deterministically on the noiseless scalar orbit (each step contracts the error by at most $\gamma$ and the anchor injection pulls the iterate back toward $x_0$; the Park--Ryu lower bound, \cite{ParkRyu2022}).
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- remark-23: body_md -->
\label{rem:median-constant}
The breakdown lemma requires a \emph{strict} majority: with exactly half of the points within distance $r$ of $y$ and the other half arbitrarily placed, no bound on the median better than the trivial one holds (two-point example $\{-R,0\}$). This is why the Hoeffding step above is stated as $\mathbb{P}(F\ge B/2)\le\vartheta/2$, giving a strict majority of good replicates; the earlier formulation ``at most $B/2$ failures'' would not suffice.
<!-- end remark-23 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:exact-general}
For known $\gamma$, the minimax oracle complexity of reaching $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-x^*}\le\varepsilon$ over the class of $\gamma$-contractions, for anchored/Krasnoselskii--Mann algorithms, is
$$\Theta\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^{2}(1-\gamma)^{2}}+\frac{\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{1-\gamma}\Bigr):$$
the upper bound is Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch} (which supplies both terms) and the lower bound is the maximum of Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-lower} on the affine subfamily ($\Omega(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}(1-\gamma)^{-2})$) and the deterministic lower bound $\Omega((1-\gamma)^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon))$ on the noiseless subclass for anchored/Krasnoselskii--Mann schemes (tight already for $\sigma=0$ within the class; Remark~\ref{rem:span-class}); the two bounds are forced on different subfamilies, and $\max\{a,b\}\le a+b\le2\max\{a,b\}$. In particular the $\ln(D/\varepsilon)$ factor in anchored analyses of \eqref{eq:anchored} is an artifact of \emph{constant} batching, not a property of the problem.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- remark-24: body_md -->
\label{rem:span-class}
The logarithmic deterministic term $\bar\delta^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon)$ in Theorem~\ref{thm:hp-bounded} and Corollary~\ref{cor:exact-general} is proved against \emph{anchored and Krasnoselskii--Mann schemes} (the class of \cite{ParkRyu2022}): for these, $\norm{x_n-x^*}\ge\gamma^n D$ holds along the noiseless orbit, because each step contracts the error by at most $\gamma$ and the anchor injection pulls the iterate back toward $x_0$. The bound is specific to this class --- it fails for the full span class of Section~\ref{sec:setting}, where arbitrary linear combinations permit extrapolation. Without the anchored restriction the term evaporates: on the noiseless affine subfamily $T(x)=\gamma x+(1-\gamma)z$, two exact probes $x_1=Tx_0$, $x_2=Tx_1$ identify the instance ($\gamma=\norm{x_2-x_1}/\norm{x_1-x_0}$ on the line through $x_0,x_1$, then $z=x_0+(x_1-x_0)/(1-\gamma)$), so the exact (noiseless) complexity of the affine subfamily is $O(1)$. The exactness claims of Section~\ref{sec:contraction} are therefore to be read within the span class; the stochastic terms are unaffected, since the affine Le\,Cam lower bound (Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-lower}) constrains any estimator.
<!-- end remark-24 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:ceiling}
Let $T$ be a $\gamma$-contraction and suppose the algorithm knows only an upper bound $\gamma_+\in[\tfrac12,1)$ on $\gamma$; write $\delta_+=1-\gamma_+$. Running the schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch} with $\bar\gamma=\max\{\gamma_+,\tfrac12\}$ in place of $\gamma$ yields
$$\mathbb{E}\norm{x_T-x^*}^2\;\le\;2\varepsilon^2,\qquad \sum_{k=0}^{T-1}m_k\;=\;O\!\Bigl(\frac{\sigma^2}{\delta_+^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{\ln(D/\varepsilon)}{\delta_+}\Bigr),$$
and this is optimal in $\delta_+$: the affine subfamily with gap $\delta_+$ forces $\Omega\!\bigl(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}\delta_+^{-2}\bigr)$ samples (Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-lower} with $\gamma=\gamma_+$). Thus a single geometric schedule, given \emph{any} certified upper bound on the modulus, attains the rate governed by that bound.
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Identical to Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch}: since $\gamma\le\gamma_+\le\bar\gamma$, the recursion $v_{k+1}\le\gamma^2v_k+\sigma^2/m_k\le\bar\gamma^2v_k+\sigma^2/m_k$ remains valid, the horizon $T$ satisfies $\gamma^T\le\bar\gamma^T\le\varepsilon/(2D)$, and the batch allocation is unchanged with $\bar\delta=\delta_+$.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- remark-25: body_md -->
\label{rem:barrier}
Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling} is the correct statement of ``parameter-freeness'': what suffices is a \emph{certified upper bound} on the modulus. Three observations show that one cannot do without modulus information in general, and explain why the natural certificate-free schemes fail.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*),leftmargin=2em]
\item \emph{Residual conversion needs $\delta$ from below.} For any contraction, $\norm{x-x^*}\le\norm{x-Tx}/\delta$: a residual test at tolerance $t$ certifies distance $t/\delta$, so soundness requires a lower bound on $\delta$, equivalently an upper bound on $\gamma$.
\item \emph{Trajectory probes bound $\delta$ on the wrong side.} Every quantity observable from finitely many probes is a function of directional moduli $\rho=\norm{Tx-Tx'}/\norm{x-x'}\le\gamma$, and hence of $1-\rho\ge\delta$: probes deliver upper bounds on $\delta$, never lower bounds. Consequently, replacing $\gamma$ by a trajectory estimate inside a soundness certificate is invalid for nonlinear maps. A concrete counterexample: $T(x_1,x_2)=\bigl((1-3\eta)x_1,(1-\eta/8)x_2\bigr)$, $x_0=(D,0)$, with oracle noise supported on the $x_1$-axis (admissible by Definition~\ref{def:oracle}). Every probe on the trajectory sees $\rho=1-3\eta$, while $\delta=\eta/8$; any scheme whose soundness rests on ``the observed gap is at least $2\eta$'' fails with probability one. Any scheme whose soundness rests on such a one-sided estimate fails in general.
\item \emph{The positive cases.} Affine maps are the exception: their modulus is visible exactly ($\rho=\gamma$ from any probe), which is why Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-upper} is $\gamma$-free (Corollary~\ref{cor:affine-theta}). For general maps, an a priori upper bound on $\gamma$ is precisely the one-sided information soundness needs, and Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling} shows it is also sufficient.
\end{enumerate}
Whether \emph{any} single algorithm achieves the affine rate simultaneously for all $\gamma$ on the general class is open; (a)--(b) rule out all residual-based schemes, and we conjecture the answer is negative in infinite-dimensional Hilbert spaces, where an alternative instance can hide its distinguishing structure in directions that are queried with low density. A formal impossibility theorem along these lines requires a resistant-oracle construction that respects the contraction constraint; we complete it in Section~\ref{sec:cert-impossibility} (Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility}), where we also show that a certified ceiling is the exact boundary.
<!-- end remark-25 -->
<!-- theorem-16: body_md -->
\label{thm:stoch-parallel}
Let $\gamma_+\in[\tfrac12,1)$ be a certified ceiling ($\gamma\le\gamma_+$ whenever the instance is a contraction), $\delta_+=1-\gamma_+$, and fix $D,\varepsilon,\sigma$. Consider the single class-oblivious algorithm that runs two tracks in parallel, each evaluated once per global step, both with deterministic coefficients and clock-based horizons computable from $(D,\varepsilon,\sigma,\gamma_+)$ alone:
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
\item \emph{Track A} (contraction track): the geometric-batch Krasnoselskii--Mann schedule of Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling} run with $\gamma_+$ in place of $\gamma$, to its deterministic horizon $T_+=\bigl\lceil\ln(2D/\varepsilon)/\ln(1/\bar\gamma_+)\bigr\rceil$;
\item \emph{Track B} (nonexpansive track): the classical Halpern iteration with batches $m_k=\lceil8\sigma^2k/\varepsilon^2\rceil$ to horizon $N=\lceil4D/\varepsilon\rceil+8$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:A1}); on a Hilbert space, track B may instead be the RAIN-based procedure of Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone-reduction}(i).
\end{itemize}
Then, on every instance, each track reaches its horizon with the following worst-case guarantees.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),leftmargin=2em]
\item If $T\in\mathcal{C}_\gamma$ with $\gamma\le\gamma_+$, the track-A output satisfies $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-x^*}^2\le2\varepsilon^2$ (Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling}), at evaluation cost $O\bigl(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}\delta_+^{-2}+\delta_+^{-1}\ln(D/\varepsilon)\bigr)$ --- optimal in $\delta_+$ (the affine Le\,Cam lower bound, Theorem~\ref{thm:affine-lower} with $\gamma=\gamma_+$).
\item If $T\in\mathcal{N}$, the track-B output satisfies $\mathbb{E}\norm{\hat x-T\hat x}\le\varepsilon$ (Theorem~\ref{thm:A1}, valid on Hilbert and $2$-uniformly smooth spaces, Remark~\ref{rem:dualization}, at cost $O(K\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-4})$; on Hilbert, $\tilde O(\sigma^2\varepsilon^{-2}+D\varepsilon^{-1})$ via Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone-reduction}), the best known in general normed spaces.
\end{enumerate}
Each guarantee is available at its own track's clock horizon, at a factor $2$ in evaluations over running that track alone. The algorithm never decides --- and, by Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility}, cannot decide from oracle data --- which guarantee applies to the instance at hand; it presents both candidates, and each guarantee is worst-case per class, which is the minimax object. In particular, \emph{given a certified ceiling, the stochastic union-class problem is solvable at the optimal contraction rate and the best-known nonexpansive residual rate simultaneously, at factor $2$ in evaluations.}
<!-- end theorem-16 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
Both tracks use deterministic coefficients and fresh batches exclusively, so every stochastic step is licensed by the measurability discipline (Remark~\ref{rem:discipline}) and no estimate-and-plug-in step occurs anywhere. Track A is exactly Corollary~\ref{cor:ceiling}: its batches and horizon are functions of $(D,\varepsilon,\sigma,\gamma_+)$ alone, and its conclusion and cost are precisely that corollary's statement. Track B is Theorem~\ref{thm:A1} (or Theorem~\ref{thm:monotone-reduction}(i) on Hilbert space). Running the tracks in parallel at one evaluation per track per global step doubles each track's evaluation count and leaves the per-class guarantees unchanged, since each guarantee depends only on its own track's trajectory.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- remark-26: body_md -->
\label{rem:stoch-parallel-open}
The open core is the factor $2$ and the second evaluation stream: whether a \emph{single}-stream algorithm (one evaluation per step) can carry both guarantees, with the contraction guarantee at the optimal $\delta_+$-rate, mirrors question (a) after Theorem~\ref{thm:parallel}. Two structural constraints, both proved above, bound what such a scheme could look like: its stopping rule cannot be a data-driven distance certificate (Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility}), and its coefficients cannot be estimated from the noisy trajectory itself without breaking the measurability discipline (Remark~\ref{rem:discipline}; cf.\ Lemma~\ref{lem:master}).
<!-- end remark-26 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:anchoring-barrier}
Let $(\theta_n)$ be any anchor schedule with $\theta_n\le 1/2$ used in \eqref{eq:anchored} with mini-batches of size $m$ --- the oracle batch averages having variance exactly $\sigma^2/m$, as for the Gaussian hard instances of Section~\ref{sec:contraction} --- on the scalar instance $T=\gamma\,\mathrm{id}$, $x_0=D$, $\delta=1-\gamma$. Then $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\le\varepsilon^2$ forces $\theta_{N-1}\le\varepsilon/D$ (in particular $N\ge cD/\varepsilon-2$ for schedules with $\theta_n\ge c/(n+2)$); and if furthermore $N\ge 1/\delta$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:stationary-lb}
\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2 \;\ge\; \frac{\sigma^2}{16\,m\,\delta}\;\ge\;\frac{\gamma^2\sigma^2}{16\,m\,\delta}.
\end{equation}
Hence accuracy $\varepsilon$ requires $m\ge\sigma^2/(16\varepsilon^2\delta)$, and $Nm\ge cD\sigma^2/(16\varepsilon^3\delta)$: an $\Omega(cD\delta/\varepsilon)$-fold barrier over the optimal $\Theta\bigl(\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2\delta^2)\bigr)$ whenever $cD\delta\gg\varepsilon$.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
The exact recursion $e_{n+1}=\theta_nD+(1-\theta_n)\gamma e_n+(1-\theta_n)\bar\xi_n$ with $\mathbb{E}\bar\xi_n=0$, $\mathbb{E}\bar\xi_n^2\le\sigma^2/m$ gives $\mathbb{E} e_N\ge\theta_{N-1}D$ (all terms nonnegative, and $\mathbb{E}\bar\xi_n=0$), where $e_N$ is read as the signed error $x_N$ (the recursion is exact on this instance and the means stay nonnegative by induction: $\mathbb{E} x_0=D\ge0$ and $\mathbb{E} x_{n+1}\ge\theta_nD\ge0$). So the hypothesis $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\le\varepsilon^2$ implies $\mathbb{E} e_N\le\varepsilon$ and hence $\theta_{N-1}\le\varepsilon/D$; for $\theta_n\ge c/(n+2)$ this forces $N+1\ge cD/\varepsilon$. For the variance bound, unfold the exact second-moment recursion $v_{n+1}=(1-\theta_n)^2\gamma^2v_n+(1-\theta_n)^2\sigma^2/m$ (the cross term vanishes by conditional unbiasedness):
$$v_N\;\ge\;\sum_{k=N-K}^{N-1}\gamma^{2(N-1-k)}(1-\theta_k)^2\frac{\sigma^2}{m}\;\ge\;\frac{\sigma^2}{4m}\sum_{j=0}^{K-1}\gamma^{2j}\;=\;\frac{\sigma^2}{4m}\cdot\frac{1-\gamma^{2K}}{1-\gamma^2},$$
using $(1-\theta_k)^2\ge1/4$. Take $K=\lceil\ln2/(2\delta)\rceil$: then $\gamma^{2K}\le e^{-2K\delta}\le1/2$ and $1-\gamma^2\le2\delta$, and $K\le N$ because $N\ge1/\delta$. Hence $v_N\ge\sigma^2/(16m\delta)$.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- theorem-17: body_md -->
\label{thm:anchoring-cost}
For the classical-schedule stochastic Halpern iteration with batches $k_n = \lceil n^2\gamma^{N-n}\rceil$ ($n\ge1$) of \cite[Corollary 4.3]{BravoContreras2024}, any run that certifies $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\le\varepsilon^2$ on the scalar instance $T=\gamma\,\mathrm{id}$, $x_0=D$, $\delta=1-\gamma$, with $D\ge4\varepsilon$, requires
$$\sum_{n=1}^N k_n \;=\; \Omega\!\Bigl(\frac{D^2}{\varepsilon^2\delta^3}\;+\;\frac{\sigma^2}{\varepsilon^2\delta^2}\Bigr).$$
The first, bias-driven term is $\sigma$-free and shows that the $(1-\gamma)^{-3}$ exponent of the $O(\sigma^2D^2\varepsilon^{-2}(1-\gamma)^{-3})$ upper bound of \cite[Corollary 4.3]{BravoContreras2024} is tight for that algorithm. The second term is the information-theoretic noise floor; the two effects do not multiply, and the classical algorithm always pays the worse of the two (whereas the geometric schedule of Theorem~\ref{thm:geobatch} attains the floor at the better exponent $(1-\gamma)^{-2}$ simultaneously with the geometric phase, cf.\ Corollary~\ref{cor:exact-general}). We formalize the mechanism behind the gap: any anchored scheme whose bias decays only algebraically pays polynomially more than the optimum of Corollary~\ref{cor:exact-general}.
<!-- end theorem-17 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
For $T=\gamma\,\mathrm{id}$, $x_0=D$, $\theta_n = 1/(n+2)$, induction on the mean-error recursion $e_{n+1}=\frac{D}{n+2}+\frac{n+1}{n+2}\gamma e_n$ gives the closed form $e_N = \frac{D(1-\gamma^{N+1})}{(1-\gamma)(N+1)}$ (with $e_0=D$), and since $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\ge e_N^2$, the hypothesis $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\le\varepsilon^2$ forces $e_N\le\varepsilon$, hence
$$N+1\;\ge\;\frac{D(1-\gamma^{N+1})}{(1-\gamma)\varepsilon}\;\ge\;\frac{D(1-\varepsilon)}{(1-\gamma)\varepsilon}\qquad\text{once } N\ge\frac{\ln(1/\varepsilon)}{\ln(1/\gamma)}.$$
(The second inequality uses $\gamma^{N+1}\le\varepsilon$, valid once $N\ge\ln(1/\varepsilon)/\ln(1/\gamma)$, since then $\gamma^{N+1}=e^{-(N+1)\ln(1/\gamma)}\le e^{-\ln(1/\varepsilon)}=\varepsilon$; note also that $e_N\le\varepsilon$ forces $N\delta\ge2$ whenever $D\ge4\varepsilon$: with $u:=(N+1)\delta$ we have $\gamma^{N+1}\le e^{-u}$ because $\ln(1/\gamma)\ge\delta$, hence $e_N\ge D(1-e^{-u})/u\ge D(1-e^{-3})/3$ --- the map $u\mapsto(1-e^{-u})/u$ is decreasing --- while $N\delta<2$ (and $N\ge2$) gives $u<3$; thus $e_N\ge D(1-e^{-3})/3\ge4(1-e^{-3})\varepsilon/3>\varepsilon$, a contradiction.)

\emph{First term (bias).} With $\gamma^N\le1/2$ (enforced by the previous display for $\varepsilon\le1/2$), we bound the batch sum over the second half of the windows:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:batch-sum-lb}
\sum_{n=1}^N \lceil n^2\gamma^{N-n}\rceil \;\ge\; \sum_{n=\lceil N/2\rceil}^{N} n^2\gamma^{N-n} \;\ge\;\frac{N^2}{4}\sum_{j=0}^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor}\gamma^{j} \;\ge\;\frac{N^2}{4}\cdot\frac{1-\gamma^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1}}{1-\gamma} \;\ge\;\frac{N^2}{4}\cdot\frac{1-1/\sqrt2}{\delta},
\end{equation}
using $n\ge N/2$ in the second inequality, reindexing $j=N-n$ in the first, and $\gamma^{\lfloor N/2\rfloor+1}\le\gamma^{N/2}\le1/\sqrt2$ in the last (since $\gamma^N\le1/2$ and $\gamma<1$). Inserting $N+1\ge D(1-\varepsilon)/(\delta\varepsilon)$,
$$\sum_{n=1}^N k_n \;\ge\;\frac{(1-1/\sqrt2)}{16}\cdot\frac{D^2(1-\varepsilon)^2}{\varepsilon^2\delta^3}\;=\;\Omega\!\Bigl(\frac{D^2}{\varepsilon^2\delta^3}\Bigr),$$
which is the first term. (\emph{Remark on the proof.} The displayed bound is obtained from the $j\le N/2$ part of the geometric sum only; the stronger-looking estimate $\sum_n n^2\gamma^{N-n}\ge N^2\sum_{j<N}\gamma^j$ is \emph{false} in general --- the middle terms $n\approx N-j$ with $j\asymp 1/\delta$ contribute negatively to the difference --- so we keep the explicit half-window truncation above, which is valid for all $\gamma\in(0,1)$.)

For the noise floor, the centered recursion is exact on this instance (all coefficients deterministic, and Gaussian noise, so $\operatorname{Var}(\bar\xi_n)=\sigma^2/k_n$ exactly): with $\operatorname{Var}_k:=\operatorname{Var}(x_k)$,
$$\operatorname{Var}_N\;=\;\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}W_n\,\frac{\sigma^2}{k_n},\qquad W_n:=\alpha_n^2\,\gamma^{2(N-1-n)},\qquad \alpha_n:=(1-\theta_n)\!\!\prod_{j=n+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)=\frac{n+1}{N+1},$$
using $(1-\theta_n)\prod_{j=n+1}^{N-1}(1-\theta_j)=\frac{n+1}{n+2}\cdot\frac{n+2}{N+1}$ (the schedule is indexed from $n=1$; if instead a single evaluation is made at step $0$, it adds $1$ to $Q$ and a variance contribution $\alpha_0^2\gamma^{2(N-1)}\sigma^2\le\sigma^2\gamma^{2(N-1)}$, exponentially small in $N\delta$ and absorbed in both terms). By Cauchy--Schwarz, for $Q:=\sum_{n=1}^N k_n$,
$$Q\;\ge\;\frac{\bigl(\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\sqrt{W_n}\bigr)^2}{\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}W_n/k_n}\;=\;\sigma^2\,\frac{S_1^2}{\operatorname{Var}_N},\qquad S_1:=\sum_{n=1}^{N-1}\alpha_n\gamma^{N-1-n}=\frac{1}{N+1}\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}(N-i)\gamma^i.$$
The inner sum has the closed form $\sum_{i=0}^{N-2}(N-i)\gamma^i=\frac{N-(N+1)\gamma-\gamma^{N-1}+2\gamma^N}{(1-\gamma)^2}$, so for $\delta\le\tfrac12$,
$$S_1\;=\;\frac{1}{\delta}\cdot\frac{(N+1)\delta-1+\gamma^{N-1}(1-2\delta)}{(N+1)\delta}\;\ge\;\frac{1}{\delta}\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{(N+1)\delta}\Bigr)\;\ge\;\frac{1}{2\delta}\qquad\text{whenever }N\delta\ge2,$$
using $\gamma^{N-1}(1-2\delta)\ge0$ and $(N+1)\delta\ge2$. Since $\operatorname{Var}_N\le\mathbb{E}\norm{x_N-x^*}^2\le\varepsilon^2$, we obtain $Q\ge\sigma^2/(4\varepsilon^2\delta^2)$, the second term (for $\delta>\tfrac12$ the first term alone dominates up to absolute constants, and the closed form gives $S_1=\frac{N-(N+1)\gamma-\gamma^{N-1}+2\gamma^N}{(N+1)(1-\gamma)^2}\ge\frac{N-2}{N+1}\ge\tfrac13$ directly).

The matching upper bound is \cite[Corollary 4.3]{BravoContreras2024}; at fixed $D,\varepsilon,\sigma$ both the lower and the upper bound scale as $\delta^{-3}$ as $\delta\to0$, so the $(1-\gamma)^{-3}$ exponent is tight for this algorithm. The final claim follows from Corollary~\ref{cor:exact-general}: any method whose analysis (or whose actual bias, as here) forces $N = \Omega((\varepsilon(1-\gamma))^{-1})$ iterations against a per-iteration variance floor pays polynomially more than the optimum $\Theta\bigl(\sigma^2/(\varepsilon^2\delta^2)+\ln(D/\varepsilon)/\delta\bigr)$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- remark-27: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{thm:anchoring-cost} identifies the exact mechanism of the gap: non-summable anchoring keeps the iterate at algebraic distance $O(1/((1-\gamma)N))$ from $x^*$ (the anchor injections are damped only over a $1/(1-\gamma)$ window), forcing $N=\Omega((\varepsilon(1-\gamma))^{-1})$; small-anchor summable-mass schedules ride the geometric KM dynamics instead (Theorem~\ref{thm:small-c}). The improvement is therefore not constant-tuning but a structural exploitation of contractivity.
<!-- end remark-27 -->
<!-- theorem-18: body_md -->
\label{thm:impossibility}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a real Hilbert space of dimension at least $1$ and fix $D,\sigma$. For every (possibly randomized) querying algorithm $\mathcal A$ with budget $M$, every certificate rule $\hat\delta$, and every $\kappa\ge2$: if $\mathbb{P}_{T_0}(\hat\delta>0)>0$ for the nominal contraction $T_0$ used in the proof below --- equivalently, for any contraction against which the scheme outputs a positive certificate with positive probability --- then for every $\eta\in(0,\mathbb{P}_{T_0}(\hat\delta>0))$ there exists a contraction $T$ on $\mathcal{H}$ with $\norm{x_0-x^*}\le D$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:unsound}
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\hat\delta>\kappa\,(1-\mathrm{Lip}\,T)\bigr)\;\ge\;\mathbb{P}_{T_0}(\hat\delta>0)-\eta\;>\;
0 .
\end{equation}
Equivalently, $\sup_T\mathbb{P}\bigl(\hat\delta>\kappa(1-\mathrm{Lip}T)\bigr)\ge\mathbb{P}_{T_0}(\hat\delta>0)$, where the supremum is over the contraction class with $\norm{x_0-x^*}\le D$. Hence no rule outputs a positive gap lower bound that is sound uniformly over the contraction class; the only universally sound certificate is the vacuous $\hat\delta\equiv0$. Distance \emph{estimation} without certificates is unaffected (Section~\ref{sec:affine-lower}) and the affine subclass is the exception where one probe reveals the modulus (Corollary~\ref{cor:affine-theta}).
<!-- end theorem-18 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Fix $\eta>0$ and, for the moment, a deterministic algorithm $\mathcal A$ (randomized algorithms are treated at the end). Run $\mathcal A$ against the nominal instance $T_0(x)=\gamma_0x$, $\gamma_0=\tfrac12$, with anchor $x_0$ satisfying $\norm{x_0}\le D/2$, and with the oracle noise of Definition~\ref{def:oracle}. Let $p_0:=\mathbb{P}_{T_0}(\hat\delta>0)$ and assume $p_0>0$ (otherwise there is nothing to prove). Choose $\delta_1\in(0,\tfrac14)$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:quantile}
\mathbb{P}_{T_0}\bigl(\hat\delta>2\kappa\delta_1\bigr)\;\ge\;p_0-\frac{\eta}{2},
\end{equation}
which is possible because $t\mapsto\mathbb{P}(\hat\delta>t)$ is right-continuous with limit $p_0$ as $t\downarrow0$ (monotone convergence over the countable partition of the range of $\hat\delta$). Set $\gamma_1=1-\delta_1$; note $\gamma_1\in(\tfrac34,1)$.

Let $q_1,\dots,q_M$ be the (random) query points of $\mathcal A$ under $T_0$; each $q_j$ is a measurable function of the noise realizations, and the distribution of each $q_j$ has at most countably many atoms. Choose finitely many points $p_i\notin\{0\}\cup\bigcup_{j=1}^M\mathrm{Atoms}(q_j)$ and pairwise disjoint balls $B_i\ni p_i$ with
\begin{equation}\label{eq:miss}
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\exists j\in\{1,\dots,M\}:\;q_j\in\textstyle\bigcup_i B_i\bigr)\;\le\;\frac{\eta}{2}.
\end{equation}
This is possible because, for each $j$ and each $p_{i}$, $\mathbb{P}(q_j\in B)\to\mathbb{P}(q_j\in\{p_{i}\})=0$ as the ball $B$ shrinks to $p_{i}$ (continuity of probability from above, using that $p_{i}$ is not an atom of $q_j$); we take the balls small enough that the finite union over $i,j$ satisfies \eqref{eq:miss}. (In dimension one the same argument holds with intervals; the complement of the countable set $\{0\}\cup\bigcup_j\mathrm{Atoms}(q_j)$ is dense.)

Modify $T_0$ inside each $B_i$: fix unit vectors $w_i$, choose amplitudes $\varepsilon_i>0$ with $2\max_i\varepsilon_i\le D/8$, and set
\begin{equation}\label{eq:bump}
T(x)\;=\;\gamma_0x+\sum_i\varepsilon_i\,\phi_i(x)\,w_i,
\end{equation}
where $\phi_i$ is the distance-function profile
\begin{equation}\label{eq:phi-def}
\phi_i(x)\;=\;\min\Bigl\{1,\;\frac{\gamma_1-\gamma_0}{\varepsilon_i}\,\mathrm{dist}(x,B_i^c)\Bigr\}.
\end{equation}
Then: (i) $0\le\phi_i\le1$ and $\phi_i$ vanishes on $B_i^c$, with $\overline{\{\phi_i\ne0\}}\subset\overline{B_i}$; (ii) $\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_i)\le(\gamma_1-\gamma_0)/\varepsilon_i$ (the distance function is $1$-Lipschitz and the minimum of $1$-Lipschitz functions is $1$-Lipschitz), and equality is attained at interior points of $B_i$ where the first branch is active, so $\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_i)=(\gamma_1-\gamma_0)/\varepsilon_i$ exactly; (iii) the supports are pairwise disjoint. We now verify that $T$ is $\gamma_1$-Lipschitz. Let $x,y\in\mathcal{H}$.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=1.5em]
\item \emph{Both outside $\bigcup_iB_i$:} $Tx-Ty=\gamma_0(x-y)$, fine.
\item \emph{$x\in B_i$, $y\in B_j$, $i\ne j$:} since $\phi_i(y)=\phi_j(x)=0$,
$$Tx-Ty=\gamma_0(x-y)+\varepsilon_i\phi_i(x)w_i-\varepsilon_j\phi_j(y)w_j,$$
so, by the triangle inequality and \eqref{eq:phi-def},
$$\norm{Tx-Ty}\;\le\;\gamma_0\norm{x-y}+\varepsilon_i\phi_i(x)+\varepsilon_j\phi_j(y)\;\le\;\gamma_0\norm{x-y}+(\gamma_1-\gamma_0)\bigl[\mathrm{dist}(x,B_i^c)+\mathrm{dist}(y,B_j^c)\bigr].$$
The segment $[x,y]$ meets $\partial B_i$ before entering $B_j$ (the balls are convex and disjoint), hence $\mathrm{dist}(x,B_i^c)+\mathrm{dist}(y,B_j^c)\le\norm{x-y}$, and $\norm{Tx-Ty}\le\gamma_1\norm{x-y}$ for \emph{every} choice of directions $w_i,w_j$.
\item \emph{$x,y\in B_i$:} $\norm{Tx-Ty}\le\gamma_0\norm{x-y}+\varepsilon_i\mathrm{Lip}(\phi_i)\norm{x-y}=\gamma_1\norm{x-y}$.
\end{itemize}
The slope $\gamma_1$ is attained inside each $B_i$ (where the linear branch of $\phi_i$ is active, in direction $w_i$), so $\mathrm{Lip}(T)=\gamma_1$. Finally, since $\norm{T-T_0}_\infty\le2\max_i\varepsilon_i\le D/8$ (the amplitudes were chosen with $2\max_i\varepsilon_i\le D/8$), the unique fixed point $x_T^*$ satisfies $\norm{x_T^*}\le\norm{T-T_0}_\infty/(1-\gamma_0)\le (D/8)/(1/2)=D/4$ (from $\norm{x_T^*}=\norm{Tx_T^*-T_0x_T^*+T_0x_T^*}\le\norm{T-T_0}_\infty+\gamma_0\norm{x_T^*}$ with $\gamma_0=\tfrac12$), so $\norm{x_0-x_T^*}\le\norm{x_0}+\norm{x_T^*}\le D/2+D/4\le D$ and the instance is admissible.

The instance $T$ is fixed before the interaction. On the event $E=\{\text{no query falls in }\bigcup_iB_i\}$ we have $T(q_j)=T_0(q_j)$ for all $j$; coupling the noise realizations, the transcript of $\mathcal A$ under $T$ then coincides almost surely with the transcript under $T_0$ --- inductively, since each query $q_{j+1}$ is a function of the transcript up to $j$, which is identical on $E$ --- so $\hat\delta$ agrees on $E$. By \eqref{eq:miss} and \eqref{eq:quantile},
$$\mathbb{P}_T\bigl(\hat\delta>2\kappa\delta_1\bigr)\;\ge\;\mathbb{P}_{T_0}\bigl(\hat\delta>2\kappa\delta_1\bigr)-\mathbb{P}(E^c)\;\ge\;p_0-\eta.$$
On the event $\{\hat\delta>2\kappa\delta_1\}$ we have $\hat\delta>2\kappa\delta_1>\kappa\delta_1=\kappa(1-\mathrm{Lip}\,T)$, which is \eqref{eq:unsound} (with $2\eta$ in place of $\eta$; since $\eta$ was arbitrary this is immaterial).

For randomized $\mathcal A$, condition on the internal random seed $R$ and rerun the deterministic construction with every choice taken from a fixed countable family under a fixed enumeration, selecting the first admissible element: the quantile $\delta_1$ is the first dyadic rational satisfying \eqref{eq:quantile} (such dyadics exist by right-continuity of $t\mapsto\mathbb{P}(\hat\delta>t)$, and admissibility is measurable in $R$); the centers $p_i$ are the first elements of a fixed countable family of distinct nonzero vectors --- which exists in every Hilbert space of dimension $\ge 1$ --- avoiding the countable set $\{0\} \cup \bigcup_j \mathrm{Atoms}(q_j)$, whose complement is dense; the radii are the first elements of a fixed countable set of positive reals small enough that \eqref{eq:miss} holds, as in the deterministic step; and the amplitudes are the first elements of a fixed countable set with $2\max_i\varepsilon_i\le D/8$. Each admissibility condition is a non-strict inequality in parameters varying measurably with $R$, so every selection is measurable, with no separability assumption on $\mathcal{H}$; the deterministic argument then gives, for every $R$,
$$\mathbb{P}_{T(R)}\bigl(\hat\delta>\kappa(1-\mathrm{Lip}T(R))\mid R\bigr)\;\ge\;\mathbb{P}_{T_0}\bigl(\hat\delta>0\mid R\bigr)-\eta .$$
Averaging over $R$ and using $\sup_T\mathbb{P}_T(\cdot)\ge\mathbb{E}_R\bigl[\mathbb{P}_{T(R)}(\cdot)\bigr]$ yields \eqref{eq:unsound} for a deterministic instance (which may depend on the law of $R$, known to the adversary).
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- remark-28: body_md -->
\label{rem:what-remains}
Theorem~\ref{thm:impossibility} concerns \emph{certification}, not convergence: Krasnoselskii--Mann with constant batches drives $\mathbb{E}\norm{x_n-x^*}^2$ to the noise floor $\sigma^2/(m\delta)$ with no modulus knowledge at all --- an algorithm can converge without ever knowing it has converged, and the theorem says the latter knowledge is exactly what costs modulus information. Together with Corollaries~\ref{cor:affine-theta}, \ref{cor:exact-general} and~\ref{cor:ceiling}, the modulus question is now resolved in all regimes: parameter-free optimal \emph{estimation} is possible for affine instances; optimal \emph{certified} solving is possible given any certified ceiling at cost governed by $\delta_+$; and without a ceiling, no positive sound certificate exists in any dimension.
<!-- end remark-28 -->
